P(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})=M^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})M(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
as a function of
\mathbf{k}
. At
\mathbf{G}=0
(red),
M(\mathbf{k},0)=U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})U(\mathbf{k})
is the identity matrix so the flat metric condition is exactly satisfied. Because the form factor
M(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
decays exponentially in
\mathbf{G}
, the flat metric condition is very nearly true for
|\mathbf{G}|\geq 3
(blue) because the eigenvalues are quite small. Thus the validity of the flat metric condition is determined to very good approximation by only the first momentum shell composed of
\mathbf{G}=\pm\mathbf{b}_{1},\pm\mathbf{b}_{2},\pm(\mathbf{b}_{1}-\mathbf{b}_{2})
(green). We see that, while
M(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
is not proportional to the identity, the differences between the eigenvalues of
P(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
are
\lesssim.33
which is only a small violation of the flat metric condition. We used the parameters
\theta=1.05
and
w_{0}=.8w_{1}
, but we checked that the flat metric condition is reliable over a range of parameters.

X
Discussion

The techniques developed in this paper allow for an analysis of general periodic Hamiltonians in
2\pi
flux — most notably the continuum models of moiré meta-materials — generalizing Bloch’s theorem in a way that allows theoretical access to non-Peierls physics. We derived formulae for matrix elements, Wilson loops and Berry curvature, and projected density-density interactions. These tools expand the reach of modern topological band theory to the strong flux limit, opening Hofstadter topology to analytical and numerical study in the continuum.

Using these techniques, we build a physical picture of twisted bilayer graphene in
2\pi
flux — a tantalizing experimental setup as the large moiré unit cell allows for laboratory access to the Hofstadter limit for intermediate and large flux
Das et al. 2022
;
Yu et al. 2021
. We find that in magic angle twisted bilayer graphene, the flat bands are reenter at
2\pi
flux after splitting and broadening into Hofstadter bands at intermediate flux. The chiral limit of TBG, although physically inaccessible, showcases the chiral anomaly and exemplifies the non-crystalline properties of Hofstadter phases.

A natural development of this work is the extension of our gauge-invariant method to study the topology of band structures at general rational flux, which we pursue in future work. Such a development would be a powerful tool to study non-Peierls physics in topological magnetic systems, particularly with the ability to perform gauge-invariant Wilson loop calculations within our formalism. Investigations of strongly correlated phases like superconductivity and the fractional quantum hall effect are also made possible due to our expressions for the form factors.

During the preparation of this work, Ref.
Sheffer and Stern 2021
independently studied the chiral limit in magnetic field. They find exact eigenstates for the zero-energy flat bands protected by chiral symmetry at
all
flux, but their techniques do not generalize to non-chiral Hamiltonians. We identify the same phase transition in Fig.
6
(e) as described in their work.

Further support was provided by the NSF-MRSEC Grant No. DMR-1420541 and DMR-2011750, BSF Israel US foundation Grant No. 2018226, and the Princeton Global Network Funds. JHA is supported by a Marshall Scholarship funded by the Marshall Aid Commemoration Commission.

Supplementary Material for “Magnetic Bloch Theorem and Reentrant Flat Bands in Twisted Bilayer Graphene at
2\pi
Flux”

The following Appendices provide self-contained, detailed calculations for readers interested in the theoretical developments described in the Main Text. Alternatively, the important computational results are highlighted for readers wishing to simply make use of the formalism. We provide a brief outline of the Appendices and summarize the final results below.

App.
A
fully develops the gauge-invariant formalism of the magnetic Bloch theorem at
2\pi
flux. App.
A.1
defines the Landau level and guiding center operators. App.
A.2
defines the magnetic translation group irreps. App.
A.3
introduces the Siegel theta function and relevant identities. App.
A.4
proves an important holomorphic/anti-holomorphic factorization formula for the Siegel theta function appearing in the basis states. App.
A.5
discusses the completeness of the magnetic translation group irrep basis states. App.
A.6
provides general formulas for the matrix elements of the magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian. App.
A.7
contains an expression for the non-Abelian Wilson loop.

App.
B
exemplifies the magnetic Bloch formalism with a simple Hamiltonian. App.
B.1
reviews Bloch’s theorem at zero flux, and App.
B.2
analogously discusses the magnetic Bloch theorem at
2\pi
flux.

App.
C
develops a gauge-invariant formula for the Coulomb interaction and an expression for the many-body form factors.

App.
D
applies the magnetic Bloch theorem to Twisted Bilayer Graphene. App.
D.1
defines the conventions of the Bistritzer-MacDonald Hamiltonian. App.
D.2
discusses the symmetries of the continuum model in magnetic flux. App.
D.3
reviews the strong coupling expansion used to study the many-body physics. App.
D.4
derives exact many-body groundstates at even integer fillings.

Appendix A
Ladder Operator Calculations and the Siegel Theta function

In this Appendix, we define the operators and special functions used to derive the magnetic Bloch theorem at
2\pi
flux. This section is technical, and the important results are highlighted for the convenience of readers. App.
A.1
contains a derivation of the symmetry operators in flux. In App.
A.2
, we prove a central formula for the magnetic translation operator eigenstates. The Siegel theta function, a generalization of the Jacobi theta function, arises in this construction and is discussed in App.
A.3
. In App.
A.4
, we derive a simple expression (related to the Green’s function of the Laplacian on the torus) for the Siegel theta function appearing as a normalization factor. With this result, we then prove the completeness of the magnetic translation group irreps in App.
A.5
. Moving on to the spectrum, we then prove a general formula for the scattering amplitude between eigenstates in App.
A.6
which is used to compute the magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian and many-body form factors. Finally, App.
A.7
derives expressions for the non-Abelian Berry connection and demonstrates that the Landau levels forming the basis carry nonzero Chern numbers.

A.1
Operator Definitions

First, we must set our index conventions. We sum over repeated indices and use greek letters
\mu,\nu,\rho,\sigma
for cartesian indices and roman letters
i,j,k,l
for the crystalline indices of the lattice vectors
\mathbf{a}_{i}
. For instance, the reciprocal lattice vectors
\mathbf{b}_{i}
are defined by
\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{j}=\delta_{ij}
, where we used the dot product as a shorthand for contracting the cartesian indices of the vectors
\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{i}
.

We recall the definitions of the canonical momentum and ladder operators defined in Sec.
II
of the Main Text. The canonical momentum with
\hbar=1
is
\boldsymbol{\pi}=-i\boldsymbol{\nabla}-e\mathbf{A}
obeying

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\pi_{\mu},\pi_{\nu}]=ie(\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu})=ieB\epsilon_{\mu\nu}

(77)

and the Landau level ladder operators
[a,a^{\dagger}]
are defined

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{x}+i\pi_{y}}{\sqrt{2eB}},a^{\dagger}=\frac{\pi_{x}-i\pi_{y}}{\sqrt{2eB}}\quad\implies\quad\pi_{x}=\sqrt{\frac{eB}{2}}(a^{\dagger}+a),\pi_{y}=i\sqrt{\frac{eB}{2}}(a^{\dagger}-a)\ .

(78)

The guiding center momenta
Q_{\mu}
are defined in a gauge-invariant way by

\displaystyle Q_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mu}-eB\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\nu}=-i\partial_{\mu}-e(A_{\mu}+B\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\nu})\ .

(79)

We note that
Q_{\mu}=A_{\mu}+B\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\nu}
can be interpreted as the canonical momentum corresponding to a system with opposite magnetic field
-B
. At zero flux, where the time-reversal is not broken,
Q_{\mu}
and
\pi_{\mu}
are identical (up to a gauge choice). We will make frequent use of the simple identity

\displaystyle(eB)^{-1}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(Q_{\nu}-\pi_{\nu})=-\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\epsilon_{\nu\rho}x_{\rho}=x_{\mu}

(80)

which gives a gauge-independent formula for
x_{\mu}
. The guiding center operators commute with the canonical momenta and obey

\displaystyle\hbox{}[Q_{\mu},\pi_{\nu}]

\displaystyle=[\pi_{\mu}-eB\epsilon_{\mu\rho}x_{\rho},\pi_{\nu}]=ieB\epsilon_{\mu\nu}-ieB\epsilon_{\mu\nu}=0

(81)

\displaystyle[Q_{\mu},Q_{\nu}]

\displaystyle=[\pi_{\mu}-eB\epsilon_{\mu\rho}x_{\rho},\pi_{\nu}-eB\epsilon_{\nu\sigma}x_{\sigma}]=-ieB\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\ .

The guiding centers form a separate oscillator system with
[b,b^{\dagger}]=1
defined by

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\frac{(\mathbf{a}_{1}-i\mathbf{a}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{Q}}{\sqrt{2\phi}},\quad b^{\dagger}=\frac{(\mathbf{a}_{1}+i\mathbf{a}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{Q}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\quad\implies\quad\sqrt{\phi/2}(b+b^{\dagger})=\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{Q},\ i\sqrt{\phi/2}(b-b^{\dagger})=\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{Q}\ .

(82)

To prove
[b,b^{\dagger}]=1
, it is useful to note
[\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{Q}]=-ieB\mathbf{a}_{1}\times\mathbf{a}_{2}=-i\phi
. From Eq. (
81
), we have
[a,b]=[a^{\dagger},b]=[a,b^{\dagger}]=[a^{\dagger},b^{\dagger}]=0
. Because
a,b
commute, we can find simultaneous eigenstates of
a^{\dagger}a
and
b^{\dagger}b
. In particular, they have a simultaneous groundstate
\ket{0}
satisfying
a\ket{0}=b\ket{0}=0
.

We define the magnetic translation operators to be

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{a}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\exp(i\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{Q})

(83)

which obey the projective algebra

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\exp\Big([i\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{Q},i\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{Q}]\Big)T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}=e^{i\phi}T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}

(84)

obtained using the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) identity
\exp X\exp Y=\exp([X,Y])\exp Y\exp X
when
[X,Y]
is a
c
-number: since
[[Q_{\mu},Q_{\nu}],Q_{\gamma}]=0
for all
\mu,\nu,\gamma
, higher-order commutators in the BCH formula disappear. Lastly, it is also useful to define the gauge-invariant
8
8

8

As the operator is built out of
\boldsymbol{Q},\boldsymbol{\pi}
operators and makes no reference to the gauge field
\mathbf{A}
, it is manifestly gauge-invariant.
angular momentum operator

\displaystyle L_{z}=\frac{\mathbf{Q}^{2}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{2}}{2eB}\ .

(85)

This choice is convenient because it decouples the
a
and
b
oscillators. To motivate the form of Eq. (
85
), we check that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{Q}^{2}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{2}}{2eB}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2eB}(Q_{\mu}-\pi_{\mu})(\pi_{\mu}+Q_{\mu})

(86)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\rho}x_{\rho}(2\pi_{\mu}-eB\epsilon_{\mu\sigma}x_{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\mu}(-i\partial_{\nu})+e\epsilon_{\mu\rho}x_{\rho}\left[A_{\mu}+\frac{B}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\sigma}x_{\sigma}\right]

and the term in brackets is pure gauge (curl-free), i.e.
\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}(A_{\nu}+\frac{B}{2}\epsilon_{\nu\sigma}x_{\sigma})=0
. Thus in the symmetric gauge
A_{\mu}=-\frac{B}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\nu}
where
\pi_{\mu}=-i\partial_{\mu}+\frac{eB}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\nu},Q_{\mu}=-i\partial_{\mu}-\frac{eB}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\nu}
, we find

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{Q}^{2}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{2}}{2eB}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\mu}(-i\partial_{\nu})

(87)

which is the canonical angular momentum operator. We now check the algebra of rotations with the magnetic translation operators. These results will be useful when we derive the real-space form of the symmetry operators in flux. The first identity we need is

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\mathbf{Q}^{2},Q_{\mu}]=Q_{\nu}[Q_{\nu},Q_{\mu}]+[Q_{\nu},Q_{\mu}]Q_{\nu}=2eBi\epsilon_{\mu\nu}Q_{\nu}

(88)

which leads to

\displaystyle\left([\frac{\mathbf{Q}^{2}}{2eB},\right)^{n}Q_{\mu}]

\displaystyle=(i\epsilon_{\mu\mu_{1}})(i\epsilon_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}})\dots(i\epsilon_{\mu_{n-1}\mu_{n}})Q_{\mu_{n}}

(89)

\displaystyle\left([\frac{\mathbf{Q}^{2}}{2eB},\right)^{n}\mathbf{Q}]

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}^{n}\mathbf{Q}

where
\sigma_{2}
is the Pauli matrix acting on the
\mu,\nu
indices. Defining
([A,)^{n}B]=[A,[A,\cdots[A,B]]],
and using an alternate version of BCH:

\displaystyle e^{X}e^{Y}e^{-X}=e^{Y+[X,Y]+\frac{1}{2!}[X,[X,Y]]\dots}

(90)

we perform the following calculation using Eq. (
89
):

\displaystyle e^{i\theta L_{z}}T_{{\bf{R}}}e^{-i\theta L_{z}}

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta L_{z}}\exp[i{\bf{R}}\cdot\mathbf{Q}]e^{-i\theta L_{z}}

(91)

\displaystyle=\exp\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\big([i\theta\frac{\mathbf{Q}^{2}}{2eB},\big)^{n}i{\bf{R}}\cdot\mathbf{Q}]

\displaystyle=\exp\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}(i\theta)^{n}i{\bf{R}}\cdot\big([\frac{\mathbf{Q}^{2}}{2eB},\big)^{n}\mathbf{Q}]

\displaystyle=\exp\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}(i\theta)^{n}i{\bf{R}}\cdot\sigma_{2}^{n}\mathbf{Q}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(i{\bf{R}}\cdot R_{-\theta}\mathbf{Q}\right)

\displaystyle=T_{R_{\theta}{\bf{R}}}\ .

This result shows that the algebra of rotations with translations is the same as at zero flux. Trivially,
[L_{z},\boldsymbol{\pi}^{2}]=0
, so
L_{z}
commutes with the scalar kinetic term. For a Dirac-type Hamiltonian, we compute directly

\displaystyle\hbox{}[L_{z}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{3},\pi_{\mu}\sigma_{\mu}]

\displaystyle=[-\frac{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{2}}{2eB}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{3},\pi_{\mu}\sigma_{\mu}]

(92)

\displaystyle=i\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\pi_{\nu}\sigma_{\mu}+i\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\pi_{\mu}\sigma_{\nu}=0

which shows that
L_{z}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{3}
is the conserved angular momentum, appropriate for a particle with Berry phase. Finally, we need the action of functions of
\mathbf{r}
to show that
L_{z}
commutes with rotationally symmetric potential terms. We use

\displaystyle\hbox{}[L_{z},x_{\mu}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{eB}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}[L_{z},Q_{\nu}-\pi_{\nu}]

(93)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2(eB)^{2}}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}([\mathbf{Q}^{2},Q_{\nu}]+[\boldsymbol{\pi}^{2},\pi_{\nu}])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{eB}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(i\epsilon_{\nu\rho}Q_{\rho}-i\epsilon_{\nu\rho}\pi_{\rho})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{eB}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(i\epsilon_{\nu\rho}(Q_{\rho}-\pi_{\rho}))

\displaystyle=i\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\nu}

which is the same action as the canonical angular momentum
[\epsilon_{\nu\rho}x_{\nu}(-i\partial_{\rho}),x_{\mu}]=-i\epsilon_{\nu\mu}x_{\mu}
. Hence a
C_{n}
-symmetric potential at
B=0
is also symmetric under rotations by Eq. (
85
) in nonzero magnetic field. This is just the classical statement that a perpendicular magnetic field does not break rotation symmetry.

A.2
Eigenstate Normalization

The calculation of the basis states in Eq. (
15
) of the Main Text is straightforward but involved. To streamline the notation, we use
\ket{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}a^{{\dagger}n}\ket{0}
to denote the Landau level wavefunctions, which obey
a\ket{0}=b\ket{0}=0
and
\braket{m|n}
. The magnetic translation eigenstates are

\displaystyle\boxed{\ket{\mathbf{k},n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2}}\ket{n}}

(94)

and
T_{\mathbf{a}_{i}}=e^{i\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}
. We have defined
{\bf{R}}=R_{1}\mathbf{a}_{1}+R_{2}\mathbf{a}_{2}
for
R_{i}\in\mathds{Z}
.

All calculations can be performed with the BCH identity
\exp X\exp Y=\exp(\frac{1}{2}[X,Y])\exp(X+Y)
when
[X,Y]
is a
c
-number. We also set
\phi=2\pi
. The first step is

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2}}

\displaystyle=e^{iR_{1}\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}e^{iR_{2}\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}=e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}+i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}}=e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})}

(95)

using
[\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{Q}]=-i\phi
. Hence we can write our states

\displaystyle\ket{\mathbf{k},n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}\ .

(96)

Recall that the
\mathbf{Q}
operators are built of
b,b^{\dagger}
operators, and
\ket{n}
is a
b
-vacuum because
a^{\dagger}
and
b
commute. We now use the oscillator variables

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\phi/2}(b+b^{\dagger})R_{1}+i\sqrt{\phi/2}(b-b^{\dagger})R_{2}=\sqrt{\phi/2}(Rb+\bar{R}b^{\dagger}),\qquad R=R_{1}+iR_{2},\bar{R}=R_{1}-iR_{2}

(97)

along with the BCH identity to compute

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=e^{-\phi/4\bar{R}R}\bra{m}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}Rb}\ket{n}=e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R}\delta_{mn}\ .

(98)

With this expression, a direct calculation yields

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})}\bra{m}e^{-i\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

(99)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})}\bra{m}e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}(R^{\prime}b+\bar{R^{\prime}}b^{\dagger})}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}(Rb+\bar{R}b^{\dagger})}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\times\bra{m}e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}(\bar{R^{\prime}}b^{\dagger})}e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}(R^{\prime}b)}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}(\bar{R}b^{\dagger})}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}(Rb)}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}}\bra{m}e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}R^{\prime}b}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\delta_{mn}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}\ .

We have reduced the calculation to a double infinite sum over the lattice vectors. A sum of this form with quadratic term in the exponential is given by a generalized theta function, called a Siegel theta function (also called a Riemann theta function). The trick to computing the sum is noticing that the sign of the imaginary terms is arbitrary because
\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}
is a multiple of
\pi
so
e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}}=\pm 1=e^{-i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}}
. Expanding out the quadratic terms, we find that

\displaystyle i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}

(100)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\phi}{4}\begin{pmatrix}R_{1}&R_{2}&R^{\prime}_{1}&R^{\prime}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&i&-1&-i\\
i&1&-i&-1\\
-1&-i&1&i\\
-i&-1&i&1\\
\end{array}\right)\begin{pmatrix}R_{1}\\
R_{2}\\
R^{\prime}_{1}\\
R^{\prime}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\mod 2\pi i

This matrix is a quadratic form has two zero modes and two eigenvalues with positive real part (not counting the
-\phi/4
prefactor) which ensures the convergence of the sum. The positive eigenvalues introduce Gaussian decay in the terms as
{\bf{R}},{\bf{R}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty
. Note that we have assumed
\phi>0
in Eq. (
78
). The zero modes disappear from the quadratic form and summing over them enforces momentum conservation as we will show. (This is identical to the Fourier transform of
1
being a delta function). We start by introducing the center-of-mass variables

\displaystyle s_{i}=\frac{R_{i}+R_{i}^{\prime}}{2},\quad d_{i}=R_{i}-R_{i}^{\prime}\ .

(101)

The
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
variables each lie on the lattice
\mathds{Z}^{2}
, and hence
\mathbf{s}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathds{Z}
and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathds{Z}
. These two variables are not independent: if
s_{i}
is half-integer, then
d_{i}
is odd, and if
s_{i}
is integer, then
d_{i}
is even. In terms of these variables, the sum in Eq. (
99
) can be simplified because the summand of Eq. (
99
) factors into
\mathbf{s}
-dependent and
\mathbf{d}
-dependent terms:

\displaystyle e^{-is_{1}(k_{1}-k_{1}^{\prime})-is_{2}(k_{2}-k_{2}^{\prime})}\exp\left(-\frac{i}{2}d_{1}(k_{1}+k^{\prime}_{1})-\frac{i}{2}d_{2}(k_{2}+k^{\prime}_{2})-\frac{\phi}{4}\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}&d_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}\\
d_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)\ .

(102)

Here
k_{i}={\bf{a}}_{i}\cdot{\bf{k}}.
In the original sum all four variables
R_{1},R_{2},R_{1}^{\prime},R_{2}^{\prime}
are all interconnected by the Riemann matrix. However, after switching to center of mass variables only the variables
d_{1},d_{2}
are connected. This is made obvious by noting the original
4\times 4
matrix can be written as a tensor product

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&i&-1&-i\\
i&1&-i&-1\\
-1&-i&1&i\\
-i&-1&i&1\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&-1\\
-1&1\end{array}\right)\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&i\\
i&1\end{array}\right)

and so its eigenstates factor. Using the center of mass variables Eq. (
101
), we can write the double sum as

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in(\mathds{Z}+\frac{1}{2},\mathds{Z}+\frac{1}{2})}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in(2\mathds{Z}+1,2\mathds{Z}+1)}+\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in(\mathds{Z},\mathds{Z})}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in(2\mathds{Z},2\mathds{Z})}+\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in(\mathds{Z}+\frac{1}{2},\mathds{Z})}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in(2\mathds{Z}+1,2\mathds{Z})}+\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in(\mathds{Z},\mathds{Z}+\frac{1}{2})}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in(2\mathds{Z},2\mathds{Z}+1)}

(111)

which separates into decoupled sums of odd (
2\mathds{Z}+1
) and even
(2\mathds{Z})
lattices. We consider the two cases. If the
s_{i}
sum is over
\mathds{Z}
and the
d_{i}
sum is over
2\mathds{Z}
, so the
k_{i}
-dependent terms can be simplified to

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{i}\in\mathds{Z}}e^{is_{i}(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime})}\sum_{d_{i}\in 2\mathds{Z}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}d_{i}(k_{i}+k^{\prime}_{i})}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)\sum_{d_{i}\in 2\mathds{Z}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}d_{i}(k_{i}+k^{\prime}_{i})}

(112)

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)\sum_{d\in\mathds{Z}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}2d(k_{i}+k_{i}+2\pi\mathds{Z})}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)\sum_{d\in\mathds{Z}}e^{-id(2k_{i}+2\pi\mathds{Z})}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)\sum_{d\in\mathds{Z}}e^{-id(2k_{i})}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)\sum_{d_{i}\in 2\mathds{Z}}e^{-id_{i}k_{i}}

If the
s_{i}
sum is over
\mathds{Z}+1/2
, then the
d_{i}
sum is over
2\mathds{Z}+1
and

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{i}\in\mathds{Z}+1/2}e^{is_{i}(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime})}\sum_{d_{i}\in 2\mathds{Z}+1}e^{-\frac{i}{2}d_{i}(k_{i}+k^{\prime}_{i})}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)e^{\frac{i}{2}(k_{1}-k_{i}^{\prime})}\sum_{d_{i}\in 2\mathds{Z}+1}e^{-\frac{i}{2}d_{i}(k_{i}+k^{\prime}_{i})}

(113)

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)e^{\frac{i}{2}(2\pi\mathds{Z})}\sum_{d\in\mathds{Z}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}(2d+1)(k_{i}+k_{i}+2\pi\mathds{Z})}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)\sum_{d\in\mathds{Z}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}2d(k_{i}+k_{i}+2\pi\mathds{Z})-\frac{i}{2}(k_{i}+k_{i}+2\pi\mathds{Z})+\frac{i}{2}(2\pi\mathds{Z})}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)\sum_{d\in\mathds{Z}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}2d(2k_{i})-\frac{i}{2}(2k_{i})}

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k_{i}-k_{i}^{\prime}\mod 2\pi)\sum_{d_{i}\in 2\mathds{Z}+1}e^{-id_{i}k_{i}}\ .

We have shown that both the even and odd type sums give the
2\pi
periodic delta function that enforces momentum conservation. Hence we arrive at

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}

(114)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\left(\sum_{d_{1}\in 2\mathds{Z}}+\sum_{d_{1}\in 2\mathds{Z}+1}\right)\left(\sum_{d_{2}\in 2\mathds{Z}}+\sum_{d_{2}\in 2\mathds{Z}+1}\right)\exp\left(-id_{1}k_{1}-id_{2}k_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}&d_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}\\
d_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\sum_{d_{1},d_{2}}\exp\left(-id_{1}k_{1}-id_{2}k_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}&d_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}\\
d_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)\ .

We will omit further mentions of ‘mod
2\pi
’ in delta functions relating momenta. The remaining
\mathbf{d}
sum is a Seigel theta function (sometimes called the Riemann theta function) defined by the quadratic form
\Phi
:

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}\left|\Phi\right.\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}e^{2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}\cdot\Phi\cdot\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right)}

(115)

which is a multi-dimensional generalization of the Jacobi theta function (see App.
A.3
). We can write our normalization factor explicitly in terms of this special function

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right),\qquad\Phi=\frac{i\phi}{4\pi}\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{pmatrix}

(116)

where we defined the matrix
\Phi
for later convenience. We now prove that
\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}\left|\Phi\right.\right)
is real for real
\mathbf{z}
for
\phi=2\pi
. This is because

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}\left|\Phi\right.\right)^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}\cdot\Phi^{*}\cdot\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right)}

(117)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}e^{2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}\cdot(-\Phi^{*})\cdot\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right)}

\displaystyle=\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}^{*}\left|\Phi\right.\right)

where in the second line we took
\mathbf{n}\to-\mathbf{n}
in the sum, and in the third line we used
\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}\cdot\Phi\cdot\mathbf{n}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}\cdot(-\Phi^{*})\cdot\mathbf{n}\mod 1
. If
\mathbf{k}
is real, then
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{k},\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{k})/(2\pi)
is real. As will be shown later in Eq. (
138
) of App.
A.3
,
\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)
for
\phi=2\pi
is non-negative for
k_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
so we can take

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})=\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\ .}

(118)

In fact, we find that
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})
has a quadratic zero at
\mathbf{k}=\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}+\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}
(we prove this in App.
A.3
), so the states at
(k_{1},k_{2})=(\pi,\pi)
are not well-defined. However, this zero must exist in the BZ for topological reasons: when a band has nonzero Chern number, it is impossible to define a smooth gauge that makes the wavefunction well-defined everywhere throughout the BZ. Our basis states
\ket{{\bf{k}},n}
correspond to Landau levels with Chern number
-1
(see App.
222
), and hence there must be a point in the BZ where
\ket{{\bf{k}},n}
is ill-defined. In our basis this occurs at
{\bf{k}}=(\pi,\pi)
, but it can be shifted arbitrarily (allowing the wavefunction to be defined in patches) by taking
\mathbf{Q}\to\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{p}
for any complex number
\mathbf{p}
. We define
\tilde{T}_{\mathbf{a}_{i}}=\exp(i\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{Q}})=e^{i\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{p}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{i}}
so that from Eq. (
94
), we see that the state

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}\tilde{T}_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1}}\tilde{T}_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2}}\ket{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{R}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2}}\ket{n}

(119)

is a properly normalized eigenstate of
\tilde{T}_{\mathbf{a}_{i}}
with an undefined point at
\mathbf{k}=\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}+\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}+\mathbf{p}
. Moreover, we will show in App.
A.6
that the zero does not impact the spectrum because magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian only has a
removable
singularity at
(\pi,\pi)
and thus is analytic in
\mathbf{k}
with a smooth spectrum.

A.3
Properties of the Siegel Theta Functions

We will prove some useful properties of the Siegel theta functions used throughout the rest of the work. Recall that the one-dimensional theta functions
\vartheta(z|\tau)
enjoy the (quasi-)periodicity relations

\displaystyle\vartheta(z+1|\tau)=\vartheta(z|\tau),\qquad\vartheta(z+\tau|\tau)=e^{-2\pi i(z+\tau/2)}\vartheta(z|\tau)

(120)

which define the function in the whole complex plane from the principal domain. We now prove the analogous properties for the Siegel theta functions.

First recall the definition of the Siegel theta function for a symmetric matrix
M
where
\text{Im }M>0
as the convergent sum:

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}\left|M\right.\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}e^{2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}\cdot M\cdot\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{n}\right)}\ .

(121)

By shifting variables in the sum, we find for any integer vector
\bf{n}

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}\left|M\right.\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}e^{2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{n})\cdot M\cdot(\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{n})-\mathbf{z}\cdot(\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{n})\right)}

(122)

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi\mathbf{n}\cdot M\cdot\mathbf{n}+2\pi i\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}e^{2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{m}\cdot M\cdot\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{n}\cdot M\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{m}\right)}

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi\mathbf{n}\cdot M\cdot\mathbf{n}+2\pi i\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}e^{2\pi i\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{m}\cdot M\cdot\mathbf{m}-(\mathbf{z}+M\mathbf{n})\cdot\mathbf{m}\right)}

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi\mathbf{n}\cdot M\cdot\mathbf{n}+2\pi i\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{n}}\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}+M\mathbf{n}\left|M\right.\right)

which gives the quasi-periodicity associated with the theta function. There is also the simpler periodicity
\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{n}\left|M\right.\right)=\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}\left|M\right.\right)
for integer vectors
\mathbf{n}
. In our application, we have
M=\Phi
(Eq. (
116
)) for
\phi=2\pi
which gives the elementary relations

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}+\frac{1}{2}(i,-1)\left|\Phi\right.\right)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi/2-2\pi iz_{1}}\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}\left|\Phi\right.\right)

(123)

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}+\frac{1}{2}(-1,i)\left|\Phi\right.\right)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi/2-2\pi iz_{2}}\vartheta\left(\mathbf{z}\left|\Phi\right.\right)\ .

We emphasize that this identity only holds at
2\pi
flux.

We now use a property known as the addition formula
DLMF, Siegel Theta
to prove that
\vartheta\big((1/2,1/2)|\Phi\big)=0
. This property is crucial for proving the Chern number of the Landau levels. Let the vector
\boldsymbol{\nu}
take values in
(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)
. The addition formula (
https://dlmf.nist.gov/21.6
) reads

\displaystyle\vartheta(\mathbf{x}|M)\vartheta(\mathbf{y}|M)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}|2M)\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|2M),\qquad\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}(\mathbf{x}|M)=\sum_{\mathbf{n}}e^{2\pi i(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{n}+\boldsymbol{\nu}/2)\cdot M\cdot(\mathbf{n}+\boldsymbol{\nu}/2)-\mathbf{x}\cdot(\mathbf{n}+\boldsymbol{\nu}/2))}

(124)

where
\boldsymbol{\nu}/2
is often called a semi-integer characteristic. This addition formula follows from straightforward algebraic manipulations of the definitions of the theta functions
Gunning 2012
. Expanding, we find

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}(\mathbf{x}|M)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}}e^{2\pi i\big(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{n}+\boldsymbol{\nu}/2)\cdot M\cdot(\mathbf{n}+\boldsymbol{\nu}/2)-\mathbf{x}\cdot(\mathbf{n}+\boldsymbol{\nu}/2)\big)}

(125)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\mathbf{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+\frac{\pi i}{4}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot M\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}}e^{2\pi i(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{n}\cdot M\cdot\mathbf{n}+(M\boldsymbol{\nu}/2-\mathbf{x})\cdot\mathbf{n})}

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\mathbf{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+\frac{\pi i}{4}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot M\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta(\mathbf{x}-M\boldsymbol{\nu}/2|M)

We now apply the addition formula applied to
M=\Phi
and
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}=(1/2,1/2)
and use the invariance of
\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)
under
\mathbf{z}\to\mathbf{z}+\mathds{Z}^{2}
and
\mathbf{z}\to-\mathbf{z}
to find

\displaystyle\vartheta((1/2,1/2)|\Phi)\vartheta((1/2,1/2)|\Phi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}e^{-\pi i(1,1)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+\frac{\pi i}{4}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot(2\Phi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta((1,1)-\Phi\boldsymbol{\nu}|2\Phi)e^{\pi i(0,0)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+\frac{\pi i}{4}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot(2\Phi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta(\Phi\boldsymbol{\nu}|2\Phi)

(126)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}e^{-\pi i(1,1)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+i\pi\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\Phi\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta(\Phi\boldsymbol{\nu}|2\Phi)\vartheta(\Phi\boldsymbol{\nu}|2\Phi)

\displaystyle=\vartheta((0,0)|2\Phi)^{2}-e^{-\pi/2}\vartheta((i/2,-1/2)|2\Phi)^{2}-e^{-\pi/2}\vartheta((-1/2,i/2)|2\Phi)^{2}

\displaystyle-e^{-\pi}\vartheta((-1/2+i/2,-1/2+i/2)|2\Phi)^{2}\ .

The next important fact is that
\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|2\Phi)=\theta(z_{1}|i)\theta(z_{2}|i)
where the Jacobi theta function is

\displaystyle\theta(z|\tau)=\sum_{\mathbf{n}}e^{2\pi i(\frac{\tau}{2}n^{2}-zn)},\quad\theta(z|\tau)=\theta(-z|\tau)

(127)

which follows because the off-diagonal term in
2\Phi
disappears:

\displaystyle 2\pi i\frac{\mathbf{n}\cdot(2\Phi)\cdot\mathbf{n}}{2}=-\pi n_{1}^{2}-\pi n_{2}^{2}-2\pi in_{1}n_{2}=-\pi n_{1}^{2}-\pi n_{2}^{2}\mod 2\pi\ .

(128)

Using the Jacobi theta functions in the result of Eq. (
126
), we find

\displaystyle\vartheta((1/2,1/2)|\Phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\theta(0|i)^{4}-2e^{-\pi/2}\theta\big(\frac{i}{2}|i\big)^{2}\theta\big(\frac{1}{2}|i\big)^{2}

(129)

where the term involving
\vartheta((-1/2+i/2,-1/2+i/2)|2\Phi)
has vanished because
-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}
is a zero of the Jacobi theta functions. We now use the sum-of-squares identity
NIST:DLMFjac
(
https://dlmf.nist.gov/20.7
)

\displaystyle\theta(0|\tau)^{2}\theta(z|\tau)^{2}

\displaystyle=\theta(1/2|\tau)^{2}\theta(z+1/2|\tau)^{2}+e^{2\pi iz+i\pi\tau}\theta(i/2|\tau)^{2}\theta(z+i/2|\tau)^{2}

(130)

which implies
\theta(0|i)^{4}=\theta(1/2|i)^{4}+e^{-\pi}\theta(i/2|i)^{4}
and hence

\displaystyle\vartheta((1/2,1/2)|\Phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\theta(1/2|i)^{2}-e^{-\pi/2}\theta(i/2|i)^{2}\right)^{2}\ .

(131)

Lastly, we use the modular identity
\sqrt{-i\tau}\theta(z|\tau)=e^{-i\pi z^{2}/\tau}\theta(z/\tau|-1/\tau)
at the self-dual point
\tau=i=-1/i
, giving

\displaystyle\theta(1/2|i)=e^{-\pi/4}\theta(-i/2|i)=e^{-\pi/4}\theta(i/2|i)

(132)

using the evenness of
\theta(z,\tau)
in the second equality:
\theta(-z,\tau)=\theta(z,\tau)
. Squaring Eq. (
132
) proves Eq. (
131
) is equal to zero. Numerically, we check that the zero is second order via the winding in Fig.
1
of the Main Text. Note that
{\cal N}({\bf{k}})
is reflection symmetric about
(\pi,\pi)
in both
k_{1},k_{2}
directions, indicating a quadratic (or higher) zero. This follows because
\vartheta\left((1/2,1/2)+\mathbf{z}\left|\Phi\right.\right)=\vartheta\left(-(1/2,1/2)-\mathbf{z}\left|\Phi\right.\right)=\vartheta\left((1/2,1/2)-\mathbf{z}\left|\Phi\right.\right)
using the periodicity and evenness in
\mathbf{z}
.

In fact, we can prove a more general identity relating
\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)
to Jacobi theta functions. Returning to Eq. (
124
), we have

\displaystyle\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}(2\mathbf{z}|2\Phi)\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}(0|2\Phi)\ .

(133)

To fit with the usual conventions for the Jacobi theta functions (see
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theta_function
), we define

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv-ie^{i\pi z+i\pi\tau/4}\theta(z+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}|\tau)

(134)

\displaystyle\theta_{2}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv e^{i\pi z+i\pi\tau/4}\theta(z+\frac{\tau}{2}|\tau)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv\theta(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(z|\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv\theta(z+\frac{1}{2}|\tau)

which are convenient parameterizations of the theta functions defined in Eq. (
127
) shifted by
0,\frac{1}{2},\frac{i}{2},\frac{1}{2}(1+i)
. These shifted theta functions will be useful when calculating the four semi-integer characteristics. It is helpful to remember that
\theta_{1}(z|\tau)
is odd in
z
and
\theta_{2}(z|\tau),\theta_{3}(z|\tau),\theta_{4}(z|\tau)
are even.

Using Eq. (
125
), we check that

\displaystyle\vartheta_{(0,0)}(\mathbf{z}|2\Phi)

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}(z_{1}|i)\theta_{3}(z_{2}|i)

(135)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{(1/2,0)}(\mathbf{z}|2\Phi)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi iz_{1}-\pi/4}\vartheta(\mathbf{z}-(i/2,-1/2)|2\Phi)=\theta_{2}(z_{1}|i)\theta_{4}(z_{2}|i)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{(0,1/2)}(\mathbf{z}|2\Phi)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi iz_{2}-\pi/4}\vartheta(\mathbf{z}-(-1/2,i/2)|2\Phi)=\theta_{4}(z_{1}|i)\theta_{2}(z_{2}|i)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{(1/2,1/2)}(\mathbf{z}|2\Phi)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i(z_{1}+z_{2})-\frac{\pi}{2}-i\frac{\pi}{2}}\vartheta((z_{1}+1/2-i/2,z_{2}+1/2-i/2)|2\Phi)=i\theta_{1}(z_{1}|i)\theta_{1}(z_{2}|i)\ .

and hence the Siegel theta function with our matrix
\Phi
can be expressed in terms of the Jacobi theta functions by plugging into Eq. (
133
)

\displaystyle\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}(2z_{1}|i)\theta_{3}(2z_{2}|i)\theta_{3}(0|i)^{2}+\Big(\theta_{2}(2z_{1}|i)\theta_{4}(2z_{2}|i)+\theta_{4}(2z_{1}|i)\theta_{2}(2z_{2}|i)\Big)\theta_{2}(0|i)\theta_{4}(0|i)\ .

(136)

No
\theta_{1}(z|i)
term appears because
\theta_{1}(0|i)=0
because it is odd. We emphasize that Eq. (
136
) relies on the precise form of
\Phi
at
\phi=2\pi
. We now need the brief identity

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{3}(0|i)^{2}}{\theta_{2}(0|i)\theta_{4}(0|i)}=e^{\pi/4}\frac{\theta(0|i)^{2}}{\theta(1/2|i)\theta(i/2|i)}=\frac{\theta(0|i)^{2}}{\theta(1/2|i)^{2}}=\sqrt{2}

(137)

which is proved using the sum-of-squares identity in Eq. (
130
) and the definitions Eq. (
134
). We simplify Eq. (
136
) to find

\displaystyle\boxed{\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)=\theta_{3}(0|i)\sqrt{\theta_{3}(2z_{1}|i)\theta_{3}(2z_{2}|i)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\theta_{2}(2z_{1}|i)\theta_{4}(2z_{2}|i)+\theta_{4}(2z_{1}|i)\theta_{2}(2z_{2}|i)\big)}\ .}

(138)

The sign of the square root above (Eq. (
138
)) is positive for real arguments
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, as we know
\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi),\theta_{3}(0|i)
are non-negative. In App.
A.4
, we give another expression for
\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)
in terms of Jacobi theta functions which is useful for numerical implementation because many common software packages, such as Python, do not implement Siegel theta (while they do implement the Jacobi theta function).

Eq. (
138
) is also useful for proving a useful property of the form factors. In Eq.
44
of the Main Text, we give an important expression for a phase factor that appears in our calculations:

\displaystyle e^{i\xi_{\bf{q}}({\bf{k}})}=\frac{e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{4\phi}}\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}{\sqrt{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}},\qquad q=q_{1}+iq_{2}.

(139)

At
k_{1},k_{2}=\pi
, the denominator tends to zero as
\sqrt{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(\pi,\pi)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}=0.
In order for this phase factor to be well defined, we must show the numerator
\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(\pi-q/2,\pi+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)=0
. We perform this calculation below.

Let
z_{1}=1/2-q/2,z_{2}=1/2+iq/2
where
q\in\mathbb{C}
. (Note that Eq.
44
generally has complex arguments in its theta functions.) Then we find

\displaystyle\vartheta((1/2-q/2,1/2+iq/2)|\Phi)

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}(0|i)\sqrt{\theta_{3}(q|i)\theta_{3}(iq|i)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\theta_{2}(q|i)\theta_{4}(iq|i)+\theta_{4}(q|i)\theta_{2}(iq|i)\big)}

(140)

where we used the elementary properties
\theta_{3}(1-q|\tau)=\theta_{3}(q|\tau),\theta_{4}(1-q|\tau)=\theta_{4}(q|\tau),
and
\theta_{2}(1-q|\tau)=-\theta_{2}(q|\tau)

NIST:DLMFjac
. For general complex arguments, there is branch cut in the square root of Eq. (
140
). This will not concern us here because we will prove both sides of Eq. (
140
) are zero.

We now use the modular identities at
\tau=i
which, using evenness, read

\displaystyle\theta_{2}(q|i)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi q^{2}}\theta_{4}(iq|i)

(141)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(q|i)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi q^{2}}\theta_{3}(iq|i)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(q|i)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi q^{2}}\theta_{2}(iq|i)

to find

\displaystyle\vartheta\Big((1/2-q/2,1/2+iq/2)\Big|\Phi\Big)

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}(0|i)e^{\pi q^{2}/2}\sqrt{\theta_{3}(q|i)^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\theta_{2}(q|i)^{2}+\theta_{4}(q|i)^{2}\big)}\ .

(142)

Once again, we use the sum-of-squares identity in Eq. (
130
) which can be written

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(0|i)^{2}\theta_{3}(q|i)^{2}

\displaystyle=\theta_{2}(0|i)^{2}\theta_{2}(q|i)^{2}+\theta_{4}(0|i)^{2}\theta_{4}(q|i)^{2}

(143)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(q|i)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{2}(q|i)^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{4}(q|i)^{2}

where we used Eq. (
137
) and
\theta_{2}(0|i)=\theta_{4}(0|i)
. Plugging in to Eq. (
142
), we find the surprising consequence

\displaystyle\vartheta\Big((1/2-q/2,1/2+iq/2)\Big|\Phi\Big)=0\quad\forall q\in\mathbb{C}\ .

(144)

Thus
\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})
(see Eq. (
44
) of the Main Text) is well-defined. We now prove that the ratio of theta functions is a pure phase, i.e. it has magnitude 1, so
\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})
is real. We need to show
e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}(e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})})^{*}=1
, which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{2\phi}}\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\bar{q}/2,k_{2}-i\bar{q}/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}

\displaystyle=1

(145)

where we used
\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)^{*}=\vartheta(\mathbf{z}^{*}|\Phi)
. Both the numerator and denominator are products of theta functions which are amenable to the addition formula. Using Eq. (
124
), the numerator of Eq. (
145
) is

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\bar{q}/2,k_{2}-i\bar{q}/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)

(146)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(2k_{1}-(q+\bar{q})/2,2k_{2}+i(q-\bar{q})/2)}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{((\bar{q}-q)/2,i(q+\bar{q})/2)}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(2k_{1}-q_{1},2k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(-iq_{2},iq_{1})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)

and the denominator of Eq. (
145
) is written

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(2k_{1}-q_{1},2k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(q_{1},q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)\ .

(147)

The first
\mathbf{k}
-dependent factors in Eqs. (
146
) and (
147
) are identical, so we only need to prove

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{2\phi}}\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(-iq_{2},iq_{1})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(q_{1},q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)

(148)

for each of the four
\boldsymbol{\nu}
. To do so, we only need to use Eq. (
135
) to write the Siegel theta functions in terms of Jacobi theta functions and use the modular identities in Eq. (
141
). It is direct to show that

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(-iq_{2},iq_{1})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\theta_{3}(-iq_{2}/(2\pi)|i)\theta_{3}(iq_{1}/(2\pi)|i),&\boldsymbol{\nu}=(0,0)\\
\theta_{2}(-iq_{2}/(2\pi)|i)\theta_{4}(iq_{1}/(2\pi)|i),&\boldsymbol{\nu}=(1,0)\\
\theta_{4}(-iq_{2}/(2\pi)|i)\theta_{2}(iq_{1}/(2\pi)|i),&\boldsymbol{\nu}=(0,1)\\
\end{cases}

(149)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi(q_{1}^{2}+q_{2}^{2})/(2\pi)^{2}}\begin{cases}\theta_{3}(q_{2}/(2\pi)|i)\theta_{3}(q_{1}/(2\pi)|i),&\boldsymbol{\nu}=(0,0)\\
\theta_{4}(q_{2}/(2\pi)|i)\theta_{2}(q_{1}/(2\pi)|i),&\boldsymbol{\nu}=(1,0)\\
\theta_{2}(q_{2}/(2\pi)|i)\theta_{4}(q_{1}/(2\pi)|i),&\boldsymbol{\nu}=(0,1)\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\bar{q}q}{2\phi}}\vartheta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}/2}\left(\left.\frac{(q_{1},q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)

which directly proves three of the four cases in Eq. (
148
). For the last case where
\theta_{1}(z|i)
is an odd function of
z
, we use Eq. (
135
) and the modular identity
\theta_{1}(z|i)=-ie^{-\pi z^{2}}\theta_{1}(iz|i)
to show

\displaystyle\vartheta_{(1/2,1/2)}\left(\left.\frac{(-iq_{2},iq_{1})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=i\theta_{1}(-iq_{2}/(2\pi)|i)\theta_{1}(iq_{1}/(2\pi)|i)

(150)

\displaystyle=-ii^{2}e^{\pi(q_{1}^{2}+q_{2}^{2})/(2\pi)^{2}}\theta_{1}(q_{2}/(2\pi)|i)\theta_{1}(q_{1}/(2\pi)|i)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\bar{q}q}{2\phi}}\vartheta_{(1/2,1/2)}\left(\left.\frac{(q_{1},q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|2\Phi\right)

which takes the same form as Eq. (
149
) and completes the proof. Hence we have shown that

\displaystyle e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}=\frac{e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{4\phi}}\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}{\sqrt{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}}

(151)

is a pure phase. This is numerically verified in Fig.
3
of the Main Text, which also shows there is a branch cut connecting
(k_{1},k_{2})
and
(k_{1}+q_{1},k_{2}+q_{2})
.

A.4
Siegel Theta as the Green’s Function on the Torus

In App.
A.3
, we found an expression (Eq. (
138
)) for the Siegel theta function
\vartheta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)
in terms of Jacobi theta functions, which we used to prove some helpful identities. We now prove a different expression in terms of holomorphic variables
k=k_{1}+ik_{2},\bar{k}=k_{1}-ik_{2}
which we need to study the completeness of the magnetic translation group basis states in Eq. (
96
). We will also show that
\log\vartheta((k_{1},k_{2})/2\pi|\Phi)
is a Green’s function on the torus
2006math......8358L
;
Mamode2014FundamentalSO
;
Polchinski:1998rq
;
francesco2012conformal
, which is essential for proving that the basis states have constant nonzero Berry curvature in App.
A.7
.

The major technical result of this section is a proof of the following claim:

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi,k_{2}-\pi)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)=\sqrt{2}\left|\theta_{1}\left(\left.\frac{k}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\theta_{1}\left.\left(\frac{i\bar{k}}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\right|\exp\left(-\frac{k\bar{k}}{4\pi}\right)\ .

(152)

We start by considering the theta function product on the righthand side. First let us state the Jacobi theta addition formula (see
https://dlmf.nist.gov/20.7
)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(0|i)^{2}\theta_{1}(w+z|i)\theta_{1}(w-z|i)

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}^{2}(w|i)\theta_{2}^{2}(z|i)-\theta_{2}^{2}(w|i)\theta_{3}^{2}(z|i)

(153)

which in concert with the modular identity
\theta_{1}(z|i)=-ie^{-\pi z^{2}}\theta_{1}(iz|i)
yields

\displaystyle-i\theta_{4}(0|i)^{2}\theta_{1}(w+z|i)e^{-\pi(w-z)^{2}}\theta_{1}(i(w-z)|i)

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}^{2}(w|i)\theta_{2}^{2}(z|i)-\theta_{2}^{2}(w|i)\theta_{3}^{2}(z|i)\ .

(154)

Upon identifying
w=k_{1}/(2\pi)
and
z=ik_{2}/(2\pi)
, we find

\displaystyle\theta_{1}\left(\left.\frac{k}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\theta_{1}\left.\left(\frac{i\bar{k}}{2\pi}\right|i\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\theta_{4}(0|i)^{2}}e^{\bar{k}^{2}/(4\pi)}\left(\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{2}^{2}(\frac{ik_{2}}{2\pi}|i)-\theta_{2}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{ik_{2}}{2\pi}|i)\right)

(155)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\theta_{4}(0|i)^{2}}e^{\bar{k}^{2}/(4\pi)}e^{2\pi k_{2}^{2}/(2\pi)^{2}}\left(\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{4}^{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}|i)-\theta_{2}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}|i)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\theta_{4}(0|i)^{2}}e^{(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})/4\pi-ik_{1}k_{2}/2\pi}\left(\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{4}^{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}|i)-\theta_{2}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}|i)\right)

where in the second line we used the modular identities for the
\theta_{2}
and
\theta_{3}
functions:
\theta_{3}(iz|i)=e^{\pi z^{2}}\theta_{3}(z|i)
and
\theta_{2}(iz|i)=e^{\pi z^{2}}\theta_{4}(z|i)
. The Jacobi theta functions
\theta_{i}(z|i)
are real for real arguments, so we conclude

\displaystyle\left|\theta_{1}\left(\left.\frac{k}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\theta_{1}\left.\left(\frac{i\bar{k}}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta_{4}(0|i)^{4}}e^{(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})/2\pi}\left(\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{4}^{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}|i)-\theta_{2}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}|i)\right)^{2}

(156)

assuming that
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Returning to Eq. (
152
) with this identity and using Eq. (
136
), the claim in Eq. (
152
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi,k_{2}-\pi)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\theta_{4}(0|i)^{4}}\left(\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{4}^{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}|i)-\theta_{2}^{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}|i)\theta_{3}^{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}|i)\right)^{2}\ .

(157)

Using the quasi-periodicities
\theta_{2}(z-1|i)=-\theta_{2}(z|i)
,
\theta_{3}(z-1|i)=\theta_{3}(z|i)
, and
\theta_{4}(z-1|i)=\theta_{4}(z|i)
from Eq. (
134
) to rewrite Eq. (
138
), we find

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi,k_{2}-\pi)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}(0|i)^{2}\left(\theta_{3}(\frac{k_{1}}{\pi}|i)\theta_{3}(\frac{k_{2}}{\pi}|i)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\theta_{2}(\frac{k_{1}}{\pi}|i)\theta_{4}(\frac{k_{2}}{\pi}|i)+\theta_{4}(\frac{k_{1}}{\pi}|i)\theta_{2}(\frac{k_{2}}{\pi}|i)\Big)\right)\ .

(158)

Recalling that
\sqrt{2}\theta_{4}(0|i)^{2}=\theta_{3}(0|i)^{2}
, the claim in Eq. (
157
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(2z|i)\theta_{3}(2w|i)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\theta_{2}(2z|i)\theta_{4}(2w|i)+\theta_{4}(2z|i)\theta_{2}(2w|i)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\theta_{4}(0|i)^{6}}\left(\theta_{3}^{2}(z|i)\theta_{4}^{2}(w|i)-\theta_{2}^{2}(z|i)\theta_{3}^{2}(w|i)\right)^{2}

(159)

where
z=k_{1}/2\pi,w=k_{2}/2\pi
for brevity. The lefthand side can be simplified with the duplication formula

\displaystyle\theta_{i}(2z|\tau)\theta_{4}(0|\tau)^{3}

\displaystyle=\theta_{i}(z|\tau)^{4}-\theta_{1}(z|\tau)^{4},\qquad i=2,4

(160)

\displaystyle 2^{3/4}\theta_{3}(2z|\tau)\theta_{4}(0|\tau)^{3}

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}(z|\tau)^{4}+\theta_{1}(z|\tau)^{4}

found in Ref.
CARLSON201142
. The identities in Eq. (
160
) allow Eq. (
159
) to be written as

\displaystyle\big(\theta_{3}^{2}(z)\theta_{4}^{2}(w)-\theta_{2}^{2}(z)\theta_{3}^{2}(w)\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(\theta^{4}_{3}(z)+\theta^{4}_{1}(z))(\theta^{4}_{3}(w)+\theta^{4}_{1}(w))

(161)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Big((\theta^{4}_{2}(z)-\theta^{4}_{1}(z))(\theta^{4}_{4}(w)-\theta^{4}_{1}(w))+(\theta^{4}_{4}(z)-\theta^{4}_{1}(z))(\theta^{4}_{2}(w)-\theta^{4}_{1}(w))\Big)

where for brevity we dropped the
i
argument, e.g.
\theta_{3}(z)\equiv\theta_{3}(z|i)
. The next step is to use the sum-of-square identities at
\tau=i
(see
https://dlmf.nist.gov/20.7
):

\displaystyle\theta_{1}^{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\theta_{4}^{2}(z)-\theta_{3}^{2}(z)

(162)

\displaystyle\theta_{1}^{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\theta_{3}^{2}(z)-\sqrt{2}\theta_{2}^{2}(z)

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\theta_{1}^{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\theta_{4}^{2}(z)-\theta_{2}^{2}(z)

which yield the identities

\displaystyle\theta_{1}^{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\theta_{4}^{2}(z)-\theta_{2}^{2}(z))

(163)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}^{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\theta_{4}^{2}(z)+\theta_{2}^{2}(z))\ .

Upon plugging in Eq. (
163
) into Eq. (
161
) so everything is written in terms of
\theta_{2}^{2}(z),\theta_{2}^{2}(z),\theta_{2}^{2}(w),\theta_{4}^{2}(w)
, it is just a matter of algebra to gather like terms and verify that Eq. (
161
) is true. Thus we have proven

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi,k_{2}-\pi)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)=\sqrt{2}\left|\theta_{1}\left(\left.\frac{k}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\theta_{1}\left.\left(\frac{i\bar{k}}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\right|\exp\left(-\frac{k\bar{k}}{4\pi}\right)\ ,

(164)

where
k=k_{1}+ik_{2}
. It is also useful to have a shifted form of the identity:

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)=\sqrt{2}\left|\theta_{1}\left(\left.\frac{k}{2\pi}+\frac{1+i}{2}\right|i\right)\theta_{1}\left.\left(\frac{i\bar{k}}{2\pi}+i\frac{1-i}{2}\right|i\right)\right|\exp\left(-\frac{k\bar{k}}{4\pi}-\frac{1}{2}(k_{1}+k_{2})-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\ .

(165)

To simplify the shifted theta functions, we recall from Eq. (
134
) that
\theta_{1}(z|i)=-ie^{i\pi z-\pi/4}\theta_{3}(z+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{2}|i)
which can be rewritten

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(z+\frac{1+i}{2}|i)

\displaystyle=-ie^{i\pi z+i\pi/2-\pi/2-\pi/4}\theta_{3}(z+1+i|i)=e^{\pi/4-i\pi z}\theta_{3}(z|i)

(166)

where in the second equality we used the quasi-periodicity of the theta functions. Plugging Eq. (
166
) into Eq. (
165
), we find

\displaystyle\boxed{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)=\sqrt{2}\left|\theta_{3}\left(\left.\frac{k}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\theta_{3}\left.\left(\frac{i\bar{k}}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\right|\exp\left(-\frac{k\bar{k}}{4\pi}\right)}

(167)

where we used
|e^{-i\frac{k}{2}-i\frac{i\bar{k}}{2}}|=e^{k_{2}/2+k_{1}/2}
. As a byproduct, Eq. (
167
) shows trivially that
\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\geq 0
, and also that the only zeros of
\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)
occur when
\theta_{3}(k/2\pi)\theta_{3}(i\bar{k}/2\pi)=0
. Up to multiplies of
2\pi
, the only zero is
k_{1}=k_{2}=\pi
because
\theta_{3}(1/2+i/2|i)=0
.

Let us now show that
\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)
is a Green’s function on the torus. This is a well-known result
Polchinski:1998rq
;
Mamode2014FundamentalSO
;
2006math......8358L
using the Jacobi theta form (the righthand side of Eq. (
152
)), but we give a self-contained argument here.

First we recall that the Laplacian on the torus
\partial^{2}=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
, where
\partial_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{i}}
for brevity, can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\partial^{2}=4\partial\bar{\partial},\qquad 2\partial\equiv\partial_{1}-i\partial_{2},\ 2\bar{\partial}\equiv\partial_{1}+i\partial_{2}

(168)

where the holomorphic derivatives satisfy
\partial k=1,\bar{\partial}k=0
. Using Eq. (
152
), we compute directly

\displaystyle\partial^{2}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi,k_{2}-\pi)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=\partial^{2}\log\left[\sqrt{2}\left|\theta_{1}\left(\left.\frac{k}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\theta_{1}\left.\left(\frac{i\bar{k}}{2\pi}\right|i\right)\right|\exp\left(-\frac{k\bar{k}}{4\pi}\right)\right]

(169)

\displaystyle=4\partial\bar{\partial}\left[\log\left|\theta_{1}(k/2\pi|i)\right|+\log\left|\theta_{1}(i\bar{k}/2\pi|i)\right|-\frac{k\bar{k}}{4\pi}\right]

\displaystyle=4\partial\bar{\partial}\frac{k\bar{k}}{4\pi}=-\frac{1}{\pi},\qquad\text{ if }k_{1},k_{2}\neq 0

where in the last line we dropped the
\log\theta_{1}
terms when
k_{1},k_{2}\neq 0
because
\partial\bar{\partial}f(k)=\partial\bar{\partial}f(\bar{k})=0
if
f
is differentiable. At
k_{1},k_{2}=0
however,
\theta_{1}(k|i)=0
so
\log\theta_{1}(k|i)
is singular and will yield a delta function contribution. Near
k_{1},k_{2}=0
, we Taylor expand
\theta_{1}(z|i)=z\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0|i)+\dots
(see
https://dlmf.nist.gov/20.4
) to compute

\displaystyle\lim_{k,\bar{k}\to 0}\partial^{2}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi,k_{2}-\pi)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=4\partial\bar{\partial}\log\big(k\bar{k}\frac{\theta^{\prime}_{1}(0)^{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\big)

(170)

\displaystyle=\partial^{2}\log(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=4\pi\delta(\mathbf{k})

using the 2D Green’s function formula
\partial^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\mathbf{k}|\right)=\delta(\mathbf{k})
. Thus we have derived the formula

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi,k_{2}-\pi)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(\mathbf{k})-\frac{1}{2\pi}

(171)

which will play a crucial role in studying the Wilson loop of a Landau level state in App.
A.7
.

A.5
Completeness Relation

In this section, we study the completeness of the magnetic translation group eigenstates
\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
defined in Eq. (
94
). So far, App.
A.2
has shown that the states

\displaystyle\ket{\mathbf{k},n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2}}\ket{n}

(172)

are orthonormal, i.e.
\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n^{\prime}|\mathbf{k},n}=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\delta_{n^{\prime}n}
, and well-defined except at
\mathbf{k}^{*}=\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}+\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}
where
\ket{\mathbf{k}^{*},n}=0
because of the Chern number obstruction to periodic states defined everywhere on the BZ. Note that the position of
\mathbf{k}^{*}
is arbitrary, and can be shifted by changing the overall phase of the magnetic translation operators (see Eq. (
119
)). In general, a complete basis satisfies

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\ket{\mathbf{k},\ell}\bra{\mathbf{k},\ell}\qquad\text{(if complete)}

(173)

where the righthand side in interpreted as a projector onto all states of the Hilbert space. Although the states at
\mathbf{k}^{*}
vanish identically and do not appear in the integral,
\mathbf{k}^{*}
is a single point and thus is a set of measure zero. Thus the missing states can be neglected when Eq. (
173
) acts on a suitable test function which is a wave packet formed from a smooth superposition of
\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
states with finite weight on each
\ket{\mathbf{k},m}
. (Note that this excludes momentum eigenstates, where an obvious pathological counterexample to Eq. (
173
) is a state of momentum
\mathbf{k}^{*}
which is projected out by the righthand side of Eq. (
173
).) Quantitatively, we consider test functions in the form

\displaystyle\ket{\rho}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\rho_{n}(\mathbf{k})\ket{\mathbf{k},n}

(174)

for a suitable smooth function
\rho_{n}(\mathbf{k})
, which excludes the case of
\rho_{n}(\mathbf{k})\sim\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{*})
. Note that
\rho_{n}(\mathbf{k})
can always be defined locally at some
\mathbf{k}
by shifting the location of
\mathbf{k}^{*}
if necessary. The rigorous conditions
\rho_{n}(\mathbf{k})
should satisfy will not concern us here. Because
\rho_{n}(\mathbf{k}^{*})
is necessarily finite and the states
\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
are only undefined at
\mathbf{k}^{*}
, we expect the completeness relation Eq. (
173
) holds up to a set of measure zero. We will now show that Eq. (
173
) is true when acting on test functions given by the continuum Landau levels
\propto a^{{\dagger}n}b^{{\dagger}m}\ket{0}
which are of the type in Eq. (
174
) because they are delocalized in momentum space.

We will prove Eq. (
173
) using the known completeness relation
Tao_1986
;
2013qher.book.....E
for the continuum Landau level operators:

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n}\ket{m,n}\bra{m,n}=1,\qquad\ket{m,n}=\frac{b^{{\dagger}m}}{\sqrt{m!}}\frac{a^{{\dagger}n}}{\sqrt{n!}}\ket{0}

(175)

where
a\ket{0}=b\ket{0}=0
. To study the completeness of the
\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
basis, we will compute an expression for

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{m^{\prime}m,n^{\prime}n}=\bra{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\ket{\mathbf{k},\ell}\bra{\mathbf{k},\ell}\right)\ket{m,n}\ .

(176)

Using Eq. (
175
), the
\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
basis is complete and Eq. (
173
) holds iff
\mathcal{I}_{m^{\prime}m,n^{\prime}n}=\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}
. The rest of this section is devoted to the calculation of
\mathcal{I}_{m^{\prime}m,n^{\prime}n}
.

We begin by simplifying the
a
oscillators in Eq. (
176
). Because the
a
and
b
oscillators commute, we easily have

\displaystyle\bra{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{\ell}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\ket{\mathbf{k},\ell}\bra{\mathbf{k},\ell}\right)\ket{m,n}=\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\bra{n}\frac{b^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}\ket{\mathbf{k},n}\bra{\mathbf{k},n}\frac{b^{{\dagger}m}}{\sqrt{m!}}\ket{n},\quad\text{ ($n$ unsummed)}\ .

(177)

The remaining overlaps can be evaluated using BCH. We expand the basis states with Eq. (
96
) to find

\displaystyle\braket{n|\frac{b^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\pi R_{1}R_{2}}\braket{0|\frac{b^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}e^{i\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{R}}|0}

(178)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\pi R_{1}R_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}\braket{0|\frac{b^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}|0}

where in the second equality we used the normal ordering identity in Eq. (
98
). We now use the coherent state identity
be^{xb^{\dagger}}\ket{0}=xe^{xb^{\dagger}}\ket{0}
to find

\displaystyle\braket{n|\frac{b^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\pi R_{1}R_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}\frac{(i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R})^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}

(179)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\frac{(-\sqrt{2\phi}\partial)^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\pi R_{1}R_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\frac{(-\sqrt{2\phi}\partial)^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})

where we introduced the holomorphic variables
k=k_{1}+ik_{2},\bar{k}=k_{1}-ik_{2}
and the holomorphic derivative
\partial
satisfying

\displaystyle\bar{R}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}=\bar{R}e^{-i\frac{k\bar{R}+\bar{k}R}{2}}=2i\partial e^{-i\frac{k\bar{R}+\bar{k}R}{2}},\qquad 2\partial\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{1}}-i\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{2}}\equiv\partial_{1}-i\partial_{2}

(180)

which obeys
\partial k=1,\partial\bar{k}=0
. The anti-holomorphic derivative
\bar{\partial}=\partial^{*}
satisfies
\bar{\partial}\bar{k}=1,\bar{\partial}k=0
. Taking complex conjugates in Eq. (
179
), we find

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k},n|\frac{b^{{\dagger}m}}{\sqrt{m!}}|n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}-i\pi R_{1}R_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}\frac{(-i\sqrt{\phi/2}R)^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}

(181)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\pi R_{1}R_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}\frac{(i\sqrt{\phi/2}R)^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\frac{(-\sqrt{2\phi}\bar{\partial})^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\pi R_{1}R_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\frac{(-\sqrt{2\phi}\bar{\partial})^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})

Returning to Eq. (
177
) with Eq. (
179
) and its Hermitian conjugate, we arrive at the expression

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{m^{\prime}m,n^{\prime}n}

\displaystyle=\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}\left(\frac{(-\sqrt{2\phi}\partial)^{m^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{m^{\prime}!}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\right)\left(\frac{(-\sqrt{2\phi}\bar{\partial})^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\right)

(182)

\displaystyle=\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\frac{(2\phi)^{(m+m^{\prime})/2}}{\sqrt{m!m^{\prime}!}}(-1)^{m^{\prime}+m}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{N}^{-1}(\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathcal{N})(\bar{\partial}^{m}\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\frac{(2\phi)^{(m+m^{\prime})/2}}{\sqrt{m!m^{\prime}!}}(-1)^{m^{\prime}+m}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{N}^{-1}\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathcal{N})(\mathcal{N}^{-1}\bar{\partial}^{m}\mathcal{N})

suppressing the
\mathbf{k}
-dependence for brevity. In the last line, we suggestively added a factor of
\mathcal{N}^{-1}\mathcal{N}
which we make use of shortly to introduce a covariant derivative structure. Eq. (
182
) has reduced the computation of
\mathcal{I}_{m^{\prime}m,n^{\prime}n}
to a single integral. Although it appears nontrivial, we can solve it exactly.

We now show that the integral can be evaluated at all
m,m^{\prime}
with an oscillator algebra of momentum space covariant derivatives to be introduced shortly. The only other result required is the integral at
m=m^{\prime}=0
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{00,00}=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i(k_{1}n_{1}+k_{2}n_{2})+\pi i\mathbf{n}\cdot\Phi\cdot\mathbf{n}}=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathds{Z}^{2}}\delta_{\mathbf{n},0}e^{\pi i\mathbf{n}\cdot\Phi\cdot\mathbf{n}}=1\ .

(183)

To perform the integral in Eq. (
182
) at general
m,m^{\prime}
, we use the form of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})
in Eq. (
167
) which reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})=\sqrt{2}\left|\theta_{3}\left(\left.k/2\pi\right|i\right)\theta_{3}\left.\left(i\bar{k}/2\pi\right|i\right)\right|\exp\left(-\frac{k\bar{k}}{4\pi}\right)\equiv\sqrt{2}f(k)\bar{f}(\bar{k})e^{-k\bar{k}/4\pi}

(184)

where for brevity we defined
f(k)=|\theta_{3}\left(\left.k/2\pi\right|i\right)|
and
\bar{f}(\bar{k})=|\theta_{3}\left(\left.i\bar{k}/2\pi\right|i\right)|
. It should be noted that
f(k)
and
\bar{f}(\bar{k})
are not complex conjugates of each other. We will sometimes suppress the arguments of
f
and
\bar{f}
for brevity.

We can now simplify the expressions for the derivatives because
\mathcal{N}
factors into holomorphic and anti-holomorphic parts, along with the exponential. First we note that the holomorphic derivative operators act in a simple way on
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})
:

\displaystyle\partial\,(e^{-k\bar{k}/4\pi}f\bar{f})

\displaystyle=e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}(\partial-\bar{k}/8\pi)(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f\bar{f})=e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\nabla(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f\bar{f}),\qquad\nabla=\partial-\bar{k}/8\pi

(185)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\,(e^{-k\bar{k}/4\pi}f\bar{f})

\displaystyle=e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}(\bar{\partial}-k/8\pi)(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f\bar{f})=e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{\nabla}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f\bar{f}),\qquad\bar{\nabla}=\bar{\partial}-k/8\pi

where we defined the covariant derivatives
\nabla
and
\bar{\nabla}
which commute:

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\bar{\nabla},\nabla]=[\bar{\partial},-\bar{k}/8\pi]+[-k/8\pi,\partial]=-1/8\pi+1/8\pi=0\ .

(186)

We also note that
\nabla\bar{f}(\bar{k})=(\partial-\bar{k}/8\pi)\bar{f}(\bar{k})=\bar{f}(\bar{k})(\partial-\bar{k}/8\pi)=\bar{f}(\bar{k})\nabla
and similarly
[\bar{\nabla},f(k)]=0
, so

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{-1}\bar{\partial}^{m}\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f\bar{f}}e^{k\bar{k}/4\pi-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{\nabla}^{m}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f\bar{f})=\frac{1}{\bar{f}}e^{k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{\nabla}^{m}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f})

(187)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{-1}\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f\bar{f}}e^{k\bar{k}/4\pi-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\nabla^{m^{\prime}}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f\bar{f}=\frac{1}{f}e^{k\bar{k}/8\pi}\nabla^{m^{\prime}}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f)\ .

Thus the integrand of Eq. (
182
) can be written

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{N}^{-1}\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathcal{N})(\mathcal{N}^{-1}\bar{\partial}^{m}\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}e^{-k\bar{k}/4\pi}f\bar{f}(\frac{1}{\bar{f}}e^{k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{\nabla}^{m}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f})(\frac{1}{f}e^{k\bar{k}/8\pi}\nabla^{m^{\prime}}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f)

(188)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}(\bar{\nabla}^{m}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f})(\nabla^{m^{\prime}}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f)\ .

The simplicity of Eq. (
188
) justifies our definition of the covariant derivatives
\nabla
and
\bar{\nabla}
. To further develop Eq. (
188
), we will need an integration by parts identity. Observe that for test functions
u(\mathbf{k}),v(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}u(\mathbf{k})\nabla v(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}(u(\mathbf{k})\partial v(\mathbf{k})-u(\mathbf{k})v(\mathbf{k})\bar{k}/8\pi)

(189)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}(u(\mathbf{k})\frac{\partial_{1}-i\partial_{2}}{2}v(\mathbf{k})-u(\mathbf{k})v(\mathbf{k})\bar{k}/8\pi)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}(-v(\mathbf{k})\frac{\partial_{1}-i\partial_{2}}{2}u(\mathbf{k})-v(\mathbf{k})u(\mathbf{k})\bar{k}/8\pi)+\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}(\partial_{1}-i\partial_{2})(u(\mathbf{k})v(\mathbf{k}))

\displaystyle=-\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}(v(\mathbf{k})\partial u(\mathbf{k})+v(\mathbf{k})u(\mathbf{k})\bar{k}/8\pi)

\displaystyle=-\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}v(\mathbf{k})(\partial+\bar{k}/8\pi)u(\mathbf{k})

where we have discarded the total derivative term because the integral is over the BZ which has no boundary. Hence we are led to define
\bar{\nabla}^{\dagger}\equiv-\partial-\bar{k}/8\pi
which satisfies

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}u\nabla v

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}v\bar{\nabla}^{\dagger}u,\qquad\bar{\nabla}^{\dagger}\equiv-(\partial+\bar{k}/8\pi)

(190)

suppressing the
\mathbf{k}
dependence for brevity. An identical calculation shows that

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}u\bar{\nabla}v

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}v\nabla^{\dagger}u,\qquad\nabla^{\dagger}\equiv-(\bar{\partial}+k/8\pi)\ .

(191)

The full algebra of the covariant derivatives is

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\nabla,\bar{\nabla}]

\displaystyle=[\nabla,\bar{\nabla}^{\dagger}]=[\nabla^{\dagger},\bar{\nabla}]=[\nabla^{\dagger},\bar{\nabla}^{\dagger}]=0

(192)

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\nabla,\nabla^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=[\partial-\bar{k}/8\pi,-(\bar{\partial}+k/8\pi)]=-1/8\pi-1/8\pi=-\frac{1}{4\pi}

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\bar{\nabla},\bar{\nabla}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=[\bar{\partial}-k/8\pi,-(\partial+\bar{k}/8\pi)]=-1/8\pi-1/8\pi=-\frac{1}{4\pi}

which form two decoupled oscillator algebra analogous to the
a
and
b
operators in real space. The last identity we need is

\displaystyle-(\nabla^{\dagger}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f(k))

\displaystyle=\bar{\partial}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f(k))+e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f(k)k/8\pi

(193)

\displaystyle=f(k)\bar{\partial}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}+e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f(k)k/8\pi

\displaystyle=-f(k)e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}k/8\pi+e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f(k)k/8\pi

\displaystyle=0\ .

In the analogy to the
a
and
b
real space algebra, we should think of
e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f(k)
as the vacuum of the
\nabla^{\dagger}
operator, and the states
\nabla^{m}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f(k))
as the (unnormalized)
m
th excited state. In an identical manner, we check that
\bar{\nabla}^{\dagger}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f}(\bar{k}))=0
.

Integrating by parts in Eq. (
188
), we find

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{N}^{-1}\partial^{m^{\prime}}\mathcal{N})(\mathcal{N}^{-1}\bar{\partial}^{m}\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{2}(\bar{\nabla}^{m}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f})(\nabla^{m^{\prime}}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f)

(194)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{2}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f})\nabla^{{\dagger}m}\nabla^{m^{\prime}}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f)

which is analogous to the correlator
\braket{0|a^{m}a^{{\dagger}m^{\prime}}|0}
. In particular, the expression
e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f}(\bar{k})\nabla^{{\dagger}m}\nabla^{m^{\prime}}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f(k))
can be evaluated with Wick’s theorem as a standard textbook computation. Wick’s theorem states that
\nabla^{{\dagger}m}\nabla^{m^{\prime}}
is equal to the normal-ordered sum of all possible contractions, where in our case the normal ordering is defined by moving all
\nabla^{{\dagger}}
operators to the right, and a contraction replaces
\nabla^{{\dagger}}\nabla
by
\nabla^{{\dagger}}\nabla-\nabla\nabla^{{\dagger}}=[\nabla^{{\dagger}},\nabla]=1/4\pi
. Then we just need the fact that any normal ordered string of operators
\nabla\dots\nabla^{\dagger}
obeys

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{2}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f}\nabla\dots\nabla^{\dagger}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f)=0

(195)

because
\dots\nabla^{\dagger}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f=0
on the righthand side and
\int d^{2}k\,e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f}\nabla\dots=0
on the lefthand side after an integration by parts because
\bar{\nabla}^{\dagger}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f})=0
(see Eq. (
193
)). Thus we find that the only nonzero term from Wick’s theorem arises when
m=m^{\prime}
and all
\nabla
and
\nabla^{\dagger}
operators can be fully contracted. Counting the
m!
possible ways of totally contracting the
m

\nabla
and
m

\nabla^{\dagger}
operators, we find

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{2}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f}\nabla^{{\dagger}m}\nabla^{m^{\prime}}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f)

\displaystyle=\delta_{mm^{\prime}}m!\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\right)^{m}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{2}e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}\bar{f}(e^{-k\bar{k}/8\pi}f)

(196)

\displaystyle=\delta_{mm^{\prime}}m!\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\right)^{m}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sqrt{2}\bar{f}(\bar{k})f(k)e^{-k\bar{k}/4\pi}

\displaystyle=\delta_{mm^{\prime}}m!\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\right)^{m}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\delta_{mm^{\prime}}m!\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\right)^{m}

where in the last line we used Eq. (
183
). We arrive at our final result by plugging Eq. (
196
) into Eq. (
182
) to find

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{m^{\prime}m,n^{\prime}n}

\displaystyle=\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\frac{(2\phi)^{(m+m^{\prime})/2}}{\sqrt{m!m^{\prime}!}}(-1)^{m+m^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}m!\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\right)^{m}=\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}

(197)

using
\phi=2\pi
. Hence we have shown that

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\ket{\mathbf{k},\ell}\bra{\mathbf{k},\ell}

(198)

is true when acting on the complete
\ket{n,m}
basis. In position space, the states
\ket{n,m}
are localized on the scale of the magnetic length
yoshioka2013quantum
, and hence we expect them to be suitably well behaved such that Eq. (
198
) does not encounter pathological cases. This is confirmed by our direct calculation.

A.6
Scattering Amplitudes

To compute the effective Hamiltonian, we need expressions for the matrix elements. Our calculations are similar to those of Landau level overlaps in the symmetric gauge, where
\psi_{l,n}(z)\propto(b^{\dagger})^{l-n}(a^{\dagger})^{n}e^{-\pi\frac{|z|^{2}}{2}}
,
l
being the angular momentum quantum number. Our states are different in that they are built from the momentum operators
T_{\bf{R}}
instead of
b^{\dagger}
directly, and are manifestly gauge-invariant.

The choice of Landau level states (which are localized on the scale of the magnetic length
1/\sqrt{eB}
) in the definition of magnetic translation irreps (Eq. (
94
)) makes the kinetic term of the Hamiltonian very simple (see App.
B
for an example), so we focus on the potential term
U(\mathbf{r})
. The potential term will create scattering between different Landau levels. Recall that
U(\mathbf{r})
is periodic so can be expanded as a Fourier series. Hence we need to compute the general scattering amplitude

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k},m|e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}\ .

(199)

with
\mathbf{G}=G_{1}\mathbf{b}_{1}+G_{2}\mathbf{b}_{2},~G_{1},G_{2}\in\mathds{Z}
. It is possible to do this exactly because
\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}
can be expressed simply in terms of
\boldsymbol{\pi}
and
\mathbf{Q}
using
-(eB)^{-1}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(\pi_{\nu}-Q_{\nu})=-\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\epsilon_{\nu\rho}x_{\rho}=x_{\mu}
. We now define

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\pi}=\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2}}\big(z_{i}a^{\dagger}+\bar{z}_{i}a\big),\quad z_{i}=\Omega^{-1/2}(\hat{x}+i\hat{y})\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i},\bar{z}_{i}=\Omega^{-1/2}(\hat{x}-i\hat{y})\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}

(200)

in terms of which we can write the Fourier harmonics

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{Q}-\boldsymbol{\pi}}{eB\Omega}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\left(i(b-b^{\dagger})-(z_{2}a^{\dagger}+\bar{z}_{2}a)\right),\qquad\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}=-\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{Q}-\boldsymbol{\pi}}{eB\Omega}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\left(-(b+b^{\dagger})+z_{1}a^{\dagger}+\bar{z}_{1}a\right)\ .

(201)

We made use of the relations
\mathbf{b}_{1}\times\mathbf{v}=\frac{\mathbf{a}_{2}}{\Omega}\cdot\mathbf{v}
and
