\mathbf{b}_{2}\times\mathbf{v}=-\frac{\mathbf{a}_{1}}{\Omega}\cdot\mathbf{v}
for any
\mathbf{v}
. This is easily verified by testing
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{b}_{2}
and using linearity of dot and cross products. The required matrix elements are in the form

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{-i2\pi\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

(202)

which can be evaluated exactly using the oscillator algebra. We now derive the normal-ordered form of Eq. (
202
):

\displaystyle e^{-i2\pi\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi iG_{1}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\left(i(b-b^{\dagger})-(z_{2}a^{\dagger}+\bar{z}_{2}a)\right)-\frac{2\pi iG_{2}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\left(-(b+b^{\dagger})+z_{1}a^{\dagger}+\bar{z}_{1}a\right)\right)

(203)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}i(Gb-\bar{G}b^{\dagger})\right)\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}((G_{2}z_{1}-G_{1}z_{2})a^{\dagger}+(G_{2}\bar{z}_{1}-G_{1}\bar{z}_{2})a)\right),\qquad G=G_{1}+iG_{2}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}i(Gb-\bar{G}b^{\dagger})\right)\exp\left(\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}+\gamma a)\right)

where in the last line we defined
\gamma=2\pi\epsilon_{ij}G_{i}\bar{z}_{j}
and we note
\gamma
is dimensionless. The exponentials separate in the last line as
[a,b]=[a,b^{\dagger}]=0.
Returning to Eq. (
203
), we use the BCH formula to find the normal-ordered form

\displaystyle e^{-i2\pi\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{4\phi}\bar{G}G}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}i\bar{G}b^{\dagger}}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}iGb}e^{-\frac{\bar{\gamma}\gamma}{4\phi}}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\gamma a}\ .

(204)

It is now a simple matter to use BCH identities to reorder the
b
oscillators to the vacuum state in Eq. (
202
). To avoid clutter, we keep
\exp\left(\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}+\gamma a)\right)
in the following expression and will normal order it at a later stage. Using Eq. (
98
), as well as
[a^{\dagger},b^{\dagger}]=[a,b^{\dagger}]=0,b\ket{n}=\bra{m}b^{\dagger}=0
, we compute

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{-i2\pi\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}\bra{m}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}i\left(Gb-\bar{G}b^{\dagger}\right)}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}+\gamma a)}\ket{n}

(205)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}e^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{4\phi}\bar{G}G}\bra{m}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}iGb}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}+\gamma a)}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}e^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{4\phi}\bar{G}G}e^{i\pi G\bar{R}}\braket{m|e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}+\gamma a)}|n}\ .

We have factored out all of the
b
operators into exponential factors depending on
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{G}
. This leaves only the
a
operators in the correlator. We use Eq. (
204
) to normal order the
e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}+\gamma a)}
term and find

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{-i2\pi\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}}e^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{4\phi}\bar{G}G}e^{i\pi G\bar{R}}e^{-\frac{\bar{\gamma}\gamma}{4\phi}}\bra{m}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\gamma a}\ket{n}\ .

(206)

We now use the Fock space identities
a\ket{n}=\sqrt{n}\ket{n-1}
to prove the formula

\displaystyle e^{xa}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}x^{k}a^{k}\ket{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{k!}x^{k}\frac{\sqrt{n!}}{\sqrt{(n-k)!}}\ket{n-k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}x^{k}\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}a^{{\dagger}(n-k)}\ket{0}\

(207)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}x^{n-k}\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}a^{{\dagger}k}\ket{0}\ .

Using the binomial formula, we find the expression

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\gamma a}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!m!}}\sum_{l=0}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\frac{i\bar{\gamma}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{m-l}\left(\frac{i\gamma}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{n-k}\binom{n}{k}\binom{m}{l}\bra{0}a^{l}a^{{\dagger}k}\ket{0}

(208)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!m!}}\sum_{l=0}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\frac{i\bar{\gamma}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{m-l}\left(\frac{i\gamma}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{n-k}\binom{n}{k}\binom{m}{l}\delta_{l,k}k!\braket{0|0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!m!}}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,m)}\left(\frac{i\bar{\gamma}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{m-k}\left(\frac{i\gamma}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{n-k}\frac{n!m!}{k!(n-k)!(m-k)!}\braket{0|0}\ .

This function may be exactly evaluated using the Laguerre polynomial definition
Abramowitz

https://secure.math.ubc.ca/~cbm/aands/page_775.htm
:

\displaystyle L^{(\alpha)}_{n}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}{\dfrac{(n+\alpha)!}{(n-i)!(\alpha+i)!}}\frac{x^{i}}{i!},

(209)

which allows us to write

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\min(n,m)}x^{m-k}y^{n-k}\frac{n!m!}{k!(n-k)!(m-k)!}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}m!y^{n-m}L_{m}^{|n-m|}(-xy),&n\geq m\\
n!x^{m-n}L_{n}^{|m-n|}(-xy),&m>n\\
\end{cases}

(210)

where
L_{n}^{\alpha}(x)
are the associated Laguerre polynomials. We finally arrive at

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\left(\gamma a+\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}\right)}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\bar{\gamma}\gamma}{4\phi}}\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{m!}{n!}}\left(\frac{i\gamma}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{n-m}L_{m}^{|n-m|}\left(\frac{\bar{\gamma}\gamma}{2\phi}\right),&n\geq m\\
\sqrt{\frac{n!}{m!}}\left(\frac{i\bar{\gamma}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{m-n}L_{n}^{|m-n|}\left(\frac{\bar{\gamma}\gamma}{2\phi}\right),&m>n\\
\end{cases}

(211)

We now have computed closed form expressions for all of the oscillator states.

Finally, we will give a formula for the matrix elements of the full momentum eigenstates. For brevity, we denote
T_{\mathbf{R}}=T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2}}
. The expression

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k},m|e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})}\bra{m}T^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}T_{\mathbf{R}}\ket{n}

(212)

can be simplified using the fact that
T^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}
commutes with
e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}
because
e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}
is periodic in
\mathbf{a}_{i}
. We then use BCH (as in Eq. (
97
)) to simplify the
T_{\mathbf{R}}
operators in the first line and Eq. (
205
) and the BCH formula in the second to find:

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}T^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}T_{\mathbf{R}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}}\bra{m}e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}e^{-i\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

(213)

\displaystyle=e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}-R_{2}R_{1}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}(R-R^{\prime})(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{4\phi}\bar{G}G}e^{i\pi G(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}\bra{m}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\left(\gamma a+\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}\right)}\ket{n}\ .

Recall that
R=R_{1}+iR_{2}
. Collecting terms, we find

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k},m|e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}

(214)

\displaystyle=\frac{\bra{m}e^{\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\left(\gamma a+\bar{\gamma}a^{\dagger}\right)}\ket{n}}{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}e^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{4\phi}\bar{G}G}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}+i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}(R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}-R_{2}R_{1}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}(R-R^{\prime})(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})+i\pi G(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}

We need only perform the sum of the
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
coordinates. We perform the same change of variables as in the normalization calculation (Eq. (
101
)) to
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{R}^{\prime})/2
and
\mathbf{d}=\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
. The calculation is identical to Eq. (
101
) but with the extra
i\pi G\bar{d}
term in the exponent which is linear and so does not enter the quadratic form. Explicitly, we have shown in Eq.
114
that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}

(215)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\ .

To evaluate the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}(R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}-R_{2}R_{1}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}(R-R^{\prime})(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})+i\pi G(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})},

(216)

we note that we can simply absorb the extra term with
G
into the momentum:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}(R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}-R_{2}R_{1}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}(R-R^{\prime})(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})+i\pi G(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}

(217)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}-\frac{\phi}{4}R^{\prime}\bar{R}^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}e^{i\pi(G,-iG)\cdot({\bf{R}}-{\bf{R}}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-(\pi G,-i\pi G))\cdot(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}-\frac{\phi}{4}R^{\prime}\bar{R}^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}

\displaystyle=\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi G,k_{2}+i\pi G)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)(2\pi)^{2}\delta(0)\ .

Returning the Landau level factor in Eq. (
211
) and using Eq. (
217
) gives the closed-form expression

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k},m|e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(0)\frac{\vartheta\left(\frac{(k_{1}-\pi G,k_{2}+i\pi G)}{2\pi}\left|\Phi\right.\right)}{\vartheta\left(\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\left|\Phi\right.\right)}e^{-\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{4\phi}\bar{G}G}e^{-\frac{\bar{\gamma}\gamma}{4\phi}}\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{m!}{n!}}\left(\frac{i\gamma}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{n-m}L_{m}^{|n-m|}\left(\frac{\bar{\gamma}\gamma}{2\phi}\right),&n\geq m\\
\sqrt{\frac{n!}{m!}}\left(\frac{i\bar{\gamma}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{m-n}L_{n}^{|m-n|}\left(\frac{\bar{\gamma}\gamma}{2\phi}\right),&m>n\\
\end{cases}\ .

(218)

Recall that
\gamma=2\pi\epsilon_{ij}G_{i}\bar{z}_{j}
,
G=(\mathbf{a}_{1}+i\mathbf{a}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{G},\bar{z}_{i}=(\hat{x}-i\hat{y})\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}/\sqrt{\Omega}
. The most important feature is the factorization of the momentum
\mathbf{G}
dependence and the Landau level
n,m
dependence. This expression can actually be simplified considerably using the quasi-periodicity of the Siegel theta functions. Let
\mathbf{n}=(G_{2},-G_{1})\in\mathds{Z}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi}-G/2,\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}+iG/2)+\Phi\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=(\frac{k_{1}}{2\pi},\frac{k_{2}}{2\pi}-G_{2})\ \quad\Phi\mathbf{n}=\frac{1}{2}(G,-i\bar{G}).

(219)

Using the quasi-periodicity property Eq. (
122
), we can transform the
G
dependence in the amplitude:

\displaystyle\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2}-2\pi G_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)=\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi G,k_{2}+i\pi G)}{2\pi}+\Phi\mathbf{n}\right|\Phi\right)

(220)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\pi\mathbf{n}^{T}\Phi\mathbf{n}-i(k_{1}-\pi G,k_{2}+i\pi G)\cdot\mathbf{n}}\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\pi G,k_{2}+i\pi G)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\ .

Remarkably, we find that the ratio of theta functions appearing in the amplitude can be written simply at
\phi=2\pi
as

\displaystyle\frac{\vartheta\left(\frac{(k_{1}-\pi G,k_{2}+i\pi G)}{2\pi}\left|\Phi\right.\right)}{\vartheta\left(\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\left|\Phi\right.\right)}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(i\pi\mathbf{n}^{T}\Phi\mathbf{n}+i(k_{1}-\pi G,k_{2}+i\pi G)\cdot\mathbf{n}\right)

(221)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{\pi}{2}G\bar{G}+i\pi G_{1}G_{2}+i(k_{1}G_{2}-k_{2}G_{1})\right)\ .

The most important feature of this calculation is the resulting exponential function in the second line of Eq. (
221
). Hence we have proven that the zero at
(\pi,\pi)
in the denominator of Eq. (
221
) has canceled (it is a removable singularity), so the amplitude is well-defined everywhere in the BZ despite the wavefunction being defined on patches. Thus the final expression for the scattering amplitude is

\displaystyle\boxed{\braket{\mathbf{k},m|e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(0)\exp\Big(-i\pi G_{1}G_{2}-i(G_{1}k_{2}-G_{2}k_{1})\Big)\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{G}}_{mn}}

(222)

and we defined the general Landau level scattering matrix in terms of
\gamma_{q}=\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}\bar{z}_{j}
and
q_{i}=\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{q}

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}_{mn}=\bra{m}\exp\left(i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\ket{n}=e^{-\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{q}\gamma_{q}}{4\phi}}\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{m!}{n!}}\left(\frac{i\gamma_{q}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{n-m}L_{m}^{|n-m|}\left(\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{q}\gamma_{q}}{2\phi}\right),&n\geq m\\
\sqrt{\frac{n!}{m!}}\left(\frac{i\bar{\gamma}_{q}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{m-n}L_{n}^{|m-n|}\left(\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{q}\gamma_{q}}{2\phi}\right),&m>n\\
\end{cases}}\ .

(223)

It is useful to think of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}
as a matrix on the Landau levels indices. It is a unitary matrix:
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}\,{\dagger}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}=\mathbb{1}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}\,{\dagger}}=\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{q}}
. The latter is easily proved from Eq. (
223
) by realizing
\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}
is a Hermitian operator which is odd in
\mathbf{q}
. To prove unitarity, we write

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}\,{\dagger}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}]_{mn}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\bra{m}\exp\left(-i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\ket{r}\bra{r}\exp\left(i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\ket{n}

(224)

\displaystyle=\bra{m}\exp\left(-i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\left[\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\ket{r}\bra{r}\right]\exp\left(i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\bra{m}\exp\left(-i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\left[\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{{b^{\dagger}}^{s}}{\sqrt{s!}}\ket{r}\bra{r}\frac{b^{s}}{\sqrt{s!}}\right]\exp\left(i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\bra{m}\exp\left(-i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\exp\left(i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\delta_{mn}

where we inserted the
s
sum of the
b
and
b^{\dagger}
operators, which commute with the
a
operators, because
b^{s}\ket{n}=0
for all
s\neq 0
, and
b^{s}=1
for
s=0
. Then we used that the
a^{\dagger},b^{\dagger}
operators form a complete set:

\displaystyle\ket{r,s}=\frac{(a^{\dagger})^{r}(b^{\dagger})^{s}}{\sqrt{r!s!}}\ket{0},~\sum_{r,s}\ket{r,s}\bra{r,s}=1.

(225)

The matrix
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}
will reappear throughout the paper. We will sometimes use the alternative representation (see Eq. (
27
) of the Main Text) where
\gamma_{q}=\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}\bar{z}_{j}
is expanded to yield

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}_{mn}=\bra{m}\exp\left(i\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}Z_{j}\right)\ket{n}=[e^{i\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}\tilde{Z}_{j}}]_{mn},\qquad Z_{j}=\frac{\bar{z}_{j}a+z_{j}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}},\quad[\tilde{Z}_{j}]_{mn}=\braket{m|Z_{j}|n}}

(226)

where
Z_{j}
is an operator and
\tilde{Z}_{j}
is a matrix on the Landau level indices. To calculate the Wilson loop expressions in App.
A.7
, we will need the commutation relations of
\tilde{Z}
matrices, which are (recall
z_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega}}(\hat{x}+i\hat{y})\cdot{\bf{a}}_{i}
)

\displaystyle\hbox{}\big[[\tilde{Z}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{j}]\big]_{mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\phi}\braket{m|[z_{i}a^{\dagger}+\bar{z}_{i}a,z_{j}a^{\dagger}+\bar{z}_{j}a]|n}

(227)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\phi}(\bar{z}_{i}z_{j}-\bar{z}_{j}z_{i})\delta_{mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\phi}\delta_{mn}\Omega^{-1}(\hat{x}-i\hat{y})^{T}(\mathbf{a}_{i}\mathbf{a}_{j}^{T}-\mathbf{a}_{j}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{T})(\hat{x}+i\hat{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\phi}\delta_{mn}\epsilon_{ij}(\hat{x}-i\hat{y})\times(\hat{x}+i\hat{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\phi}\delta_{mn}\epsilon_{ij}

which we can write in matrix notation as
[\tilde{Z}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{j}]=\mathbb{1}\frac{i}{\phi}\epsilon_{ij}
where
\mathbb{1}
acts on the Landau levels and we use
\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{u}
as well as the cross product identity

\displaystyle\epsilon_{ij}[\mathbf{a}_{i}\mathbf{a}_{j}^{T}-\mathbf{a}_{j}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{T}]_{\mu\nu}=\Omega\epsilon_{\mu\nu}

(228)

which follows from the Levi-Civita identities for matrix determinants.

With minor modifications, we can compute a more general correlator:
\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}
where
\mathbf{q}
is an arbitrary momentum in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
which may connect different momenta
\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\in BZ
. This calculation will be essential for the many-body form factors in App.
C
.

We follow the same steps as in Eq. (
212
). We first expand out the basis states:

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\bra{m}T^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}T_{\mathbf{R}}\ket{n}

(229)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\bra{m}e^{[-i{\bf{R}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{Q}},-i{\bf{q}}\cdot{\bf{r}}]}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}T^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}T_{\mathbf{R}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q})\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\bra{m}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}T^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}T_{\mathbf{R}}\ket{n}\ .

The correlator is expanded following the same steps in Eq. (
213
), replacing
\mathbf{G}\rightarrow\frac{{\bf{q}}}{2\pi}
:

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}T^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}T_{\mathbf{R}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}}\bra{m}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}e^{-i\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

(230)

\displaystyle=e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}(R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}-R_{2}R_{1}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}(R-R^{\prime})(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}e^{i\frac{q}{2}(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{4\phi}}\bra{m}e^{i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}}\ket{n}\ .

The final two factors do not depend on
\mathbf{R}
and factor out of the sum. The sum itself reads

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q})\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\bra{m}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}T^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}T_{\mathbf{R}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q})\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}(R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}-R_{2}R_{1}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}(R-R^{\prime})(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}e^{i\frac{q}{2}(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{4\phi}}\bra{m}e^{i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}}\ket{n}

which is in theta function form. As in Eq. (
213
), the additional
q
dependence can be absorbed into the momentum dependence:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+{\bf{q}})\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}(R_{1}R_{2}^{\prime}-R_{2}R_{1}^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{\phi}{4}(R-R^{\prime})(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})+i\frac{q}{2}(\bar{R}-\bar{R}^{\prime})}

(231)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+{\bf{q}})\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}-\frac{\phi}{4}R^{\prime}\bar{R}^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}e^{i(\frac{q}{2},-i\frac{q}{2})\cdot({\bf{R}}-{\bf{R}}^{\prime})}

(232)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-(\frac{q}{2},-i\frac{q}{2}))\cdot\mathbf{R}+i(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+{\bf{q}}-(\frac{q}{2},-i\frac{q}{2}))\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}_{1}R^{\prime}_{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}R\bar{R}-\frac{\phi}{4}R^{\prime}\bar{R}^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}

(233)

\displaystyle=\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)(2\pi)^{2}\delta({\bf{k}}-{\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{q}})

(234)

When
{\bf{k}}={\bf{k}}^{\prime}
and
{\bf{q}}=2\pi\mathbf{G}
, this expression must reduce to Eq. (
217
), which indeed it does. Note that the delta function is defined modulo a reciprocal lattice vector
2\pi\mathbf{G}
. We arrive at the formula

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k^{\prime}}-\mathbf{q})\frac{e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{4\phi}}\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}{\sqrt{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}}\bra{m}e^{i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}}\ket{n}

(235)

\displaystyle\equiv(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k^{\prime}}-\mathbf{q})e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}_{mn}\ .

Note that the operator
e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}
is unitary acting on position-space wavefunctions. Its representation on the
\ket{\mathbf{k},m}
basis is also unitary, as must be the case, which is proved by showing
e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}
is a pure phase (see Eq. (
224
)) and that
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}_{mn}
is a unitary matrix on the Landau level indices (see Eq. (
139
)).

A.7
Berry Connection and the Chern Number of a Lattice Landau level

Our basis of magnetic translation eigenstates is built from continuum Landau levels. These states are known to carry a Chern number, and it will be important to see how this arises in our formalism. To do this carefully, we need to compute the continuum Berry connection

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\mathcal{A}^{mn}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|\mathbf{r}|\mathbf{k},n}\ .

(236)

We will calculate the expression directly using Eq. (
203
). Using the identity

\displaystyle x_{\mu}=(eB)^{-1}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(Q_{\nu}-\pi_{\nu}),

(237)

the position operator
\mathbf{r}
can be expressed in terms of the oscillator operators via Eq. (
201
) which reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\Big(i(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})-(\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}+\gamma_{q}a)\Big),\qquad q=(\mathbf{a}_{1}+i\mathbf{a}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{q},\quad\gamma_{q}=\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}\bar{z}_{j}

(238)

where we have introduced the
arbitrary
vector
\mathbf{q}
as a technical device to aid the calculation. First we will show that the
a,a^{\dagger}
terms give a simple term:

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}+\gamma_{q}a}{\sqrt{2\phi}}|\mathbf{k},n}=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}\braket{m|Z_{j}|n}=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}[\tilde{Z}_{j}]_{mn},

(239)

using Eqs. (
223
) and (
226
). Note that, interestingly,
\exp\left(i\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}[\tilde{Z}_{j}]_{mn}\right)={\cal H}^{\bf{q}}_{mn}
. Note that Eq. (
239
) gives a contribution to
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{k})
which is independent of
\mathbf{k}
and hence will not contribute to the Berry curvature or any winding when we consider only a single Landau level. However, it will be important for multi-band effects as we consider afterwards. Physically, the
1/\sqrt{\phi}
dependence of this term signals a contribution due to the magnetic length. It is a non-Abelian term because localizing an electron below the magnetic length requires many Landau levels, implying that many other different bands
n\neq m
contribute to the Berry curvature of a band
m
.

Moving onwards, we consider the
b,b^{\dagger}
oscillators in
\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}
. Using Eq. (
96
), we need to compute

\displaystyle\bra{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}i(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})\ket{\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}^{\prime}R_{2}^{\prime}}\bra{m}e^{-i\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}i(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}\ .

(240)

Using the coherent state identities
be^{xb^{\dagger}}\ket{0}=xe^{xb^{\dagger}}\ket{0}
, as well as BCH, this correlator can be evaluated as

\displaystyle\bra{m}e^{-i\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}i(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\delta_{mn}e^{-\phi/4\bar{R}R-\phi/4\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}}\braket{0|e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}^{\prime}b^{\dagger}}e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}R^{\prime}b}i(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}Rb}|0}

(241)

\displaystyle=\delta_{mn}e^{-\phi/4\bar{R}R-\phi/4\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}}\braket{0|e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}R^{\prime}b}i(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}|0}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\phi/2}\delta_{mn}e^{-\phi/4\bar{R}R-\phi/4\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}}\Big(\bar{q}R^{\prime}+q\bar{R}\Big)\braket{0|e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}R^{\prime}b}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}|0}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\phi/2}\delta_{mn}e^{-\phi/4\bar{R}R-\phi/4\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}}\Big(\bar{q}R^{\prime}+q\bar{R}\Big)\braket{0|e^{\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}[b,b^{\dagger}]}e^{i\sqrt{\phi/2}\bar{R}b^{\dagger}}e^{-i\sqrt{\phi/2}R^{\prime}b}|0}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\phi/2}\delta_{mn}e^{-\phi/4\bar{R}R-\phi/4\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}\Big(\bar{q}R^{\prime}+q\bar{R}\Big).

We are left with an infinite sum:

\displaystyle\bra{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}\frac{i}{\sqrt{\phi/2}}(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})\ket{\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\delta_{mn}}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}(\bar{q}R^{\prime}+q\bar{R})e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}^{\prime}R_{2}^{\prime}-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}

(242)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\delta_{mn}}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}(-2\bar{q}i\bar{\partial}^{\prime}+2iq\partial)e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}-i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}^{\prime}R_{2}^{\prime}-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}\ .

Here we have used the holomorphic variables
\bar{R},R
, and momentum derivatives
\partial,\bar{\partial}
in Eq. (
180
) which we reproduce for convenience below:

\displaystyle\bar{R}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}=\bar{R}e^{-i\frac{k\bar{R}+\bar{k}R}{2}}=2i\partial e^{-i\frac{k\bar{R}+\bar{k}R}{2}},\qquad 2\partial\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{1}}-i\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{2}}\equiv\partial_{1}-i\partial_{2}

(243)

and similarly for the anti-holomorphic derivative
\bar{\partial}=\partial^{*}
which satisfy
\partial k=\bar{\partial}\bar{k}=1
. The primed derivatives act on the
k^{\prime}
and
\bar{k}^{\prime}
. Taking the derivatives out of the sum (which is easily justified because it converges absolutely) leaves Eq. (
242
) in terms of exactly the theta function expression in Eq. (
99
):

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}R_{2}+i\frac{\phi}{2}R_{1}^{\prime}R_{2}^{\prime}-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}R-\frac{\phi}{4}\bar{R}^{\prime}R^{\prime}+\frac{\phi}{2}R^{\prime}\bar{R}}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime})/2}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\ .

(244)

It is convenient to keep the symmetrized argument
(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})/2
as appears in Eq. (
102
) because we need to take derivatives of the Dirac delta function. Explicitly, we must calculate

\displaystyle\bra{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})\ket{\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{mn}(2\pi)^{2}}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}}i(\bar{q}\bar{\partial}^{\prime}-q\partial)\left[\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}/2+\mathbf{k}/2)\right],\qquad\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})=\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\ .

(245)

Note the
\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}
prefactor in this expression compared to Eq. (
242
).

Expanding the derivatives yields

\displaystyle i(\bar{q}\bar{\partial}^{\prime}-q\partial)\left[\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\mathcal{N}\left(\frac{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{k}}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\frac{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{k}}{2}\right)i(\bar{q}\bar{\partial}^{\prime}-q\partial)\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})+\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})i(\bar{q}\bar{\partial}^{\prime}-q\partial)\left[\mathcal{N}\left(\frac{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{k}}{2}\right)\right]

(246)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}\left(\frac{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{k}}{2}\right)i(-\bar{q}\bar{\partial}-q\partial)\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})i\left.(\bar{q}\bar{\partial}_{z}-q\partial_{z})\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{z})\right|_{\mathbf{z}=\frac{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{k}}{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})i(-\bar{q}\bar{\partial}-q\partial)\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})i(\bar{q}\bar{\partial}-q\partial)\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})

where we have used the chain rule to rewrite the
\partial^{\prime}
operators in terms of
\partial
and then set
\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
after performing the derivatives. The manipulation in the second line uses the chain rule to write, e.g.
\partial_{k}[f(\frac{k+k^{\prime}}{2})]=f^{\prime}(\frac{k+k^{\prime}}{2})\partial_{k}\frac{k+k^{\prime}}{2}=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime}(\frac{k+k^{\prime}}{2})
where as usual
f^{\prime}(x)=\partial_{x}f(x)
.

Finally, we have the following identities

\displaystyle\bar{q}\bar{\partial}+q\partial=q_{i}\partial_{i},\quad i(\bar{q}\bar{\partial}-q\partial)=-\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}\partial_{j}

(247)

which give the expression

\displaystyle\bra{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(qb-\bar{q}b^{\dagger})\ket{\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\delta_{mn}(2\pi)^{2}q_{i}\Big(-i\partial_{i}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{j}\log\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\Big)\ .

(248)

The two terms in this expression have different physical consequences. The first term appears in the Berry connection at zero flux:

\displaystyle\int d^{2}r\ e^{-i(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}-\mathbf{G}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\mathbf{r}\,e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{r}}

\displaystyle=(-i\partial_{\mathbf{k}})\int d^{2}r\ e^{-i(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}-\mathbf{G}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{r}}\mathbf{r}\,e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{r}}=-i(2\pi)^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}\partial_{\mathbf{k}}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})

(249)

where the functions
e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{r}}
are a basis of the Bloch states at zero flux. When there are nontrivial Bloch functions, the
-i\partial_{\mathbf{k}}
is responsible for the conventional
U^{\dagger}(i\partial_{\mathbf{k}})U
contribution to the Berry curvature where
U(\mathbf{k})
are the matrix eigenvectors. To see this, recall that the Bloch eigenstates in the
M
th band are defined

\displaystyle\ket{{\bf{k}},M}_{\phi=0}=\sum_{m}\ket{{\bf{k}},\mathbf{G}}U^{M}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf{k}}),

(250)

so the Berry connection in this basis yields

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf{k}}^{\prime},M|{\bf{r}}|{\bf{k}},N}_{\phi=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}[U^{\dagger}({\bf{k}}^{\prime})]^{M}_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}{\braket{{\bf{k}}^{\prime},\mathbf{G}^{\prime}|{\bf{r}}|{\bf{k}},\mathbf{G}}U^{N}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf{k}})}

(251)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}[U^{\dagger}({\bf{k}}^{\prime})]^{M}_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}[-i(2\pi)^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}\mathbf{G}}\partial_{\bf{k}}\delta({\bf{k}}-{\bf{k}}^{\prime})]U^{N}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf{k}})

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta({\bf{k}}-{\bf{k}}^{\prime})\sum_{\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}[U^{\dagger}({\bf{k}}^{\prime})]^{M}_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}[\delta_{\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}i\partial_{\bf{k}}U^{N}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf{k}})]

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta({\bf{k}}-{\bf{k}}^{\prime})[U^{\dagger}({\bf{k}})(i\partial_{\bf{k}})U({\bf{k}})]^{MN}\ .

where we used the delta function identity
f(\mathbf{k})\partial_{\mathbf{k}}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})=-\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\partial_{\mathbf{k}}f(\mathbf{k})
. If the basis includes orbital degrees of freedom carrying Landau levels, such as sublattice or layer as in TBG, then Eq. (
251
) should be summed over the orbital indices as well.

The
\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{j}\log\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})
term in Eq. (
248
) has no zero-flux analogue. It arises entirely from the normalization factor
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})
. This term is responsible for the nonzero Chern number of the basis states as we now show. The Abelian Berry connection of the
m
th Landau level is independent of
m
and is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{mm}=\mathbf{b}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{A}^{mm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{j}\log\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(252)

Using Eq. (
171
), we have the following fact about the Abelian Berry curvature:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{i}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{mm}=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{jk}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}\log\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}\log\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}+2\pi\delta(\mathbf{k}-\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}-\pi\mathbf{b}_{2})

(253)

which shows that there are two contributions to the Berry curvature. The first term
-\frac{1}{2\pi}
is the perfectly flat Berry curvature expected for a Landau level
Wang et al. 2021
. Integrating
-\frac{1}{2\pi}
over the BZ of area
(2\pi)^{2}
gives
-2\pi
corresponding to a Chern number
C=-1
. The second term in Eq. (
253
) is a singular delta function contribution to the Berry curvature which is appears at the singular point
\mathbf{k}^{*}=\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}+\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}
where
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{*})=0
and
\ket{\mathbf{k}^{*},n}
is undefined. Note that
\mathbf{k}^{*}
can be chosen arbitrarily via a gauge transformation (see Eq. (
119
)) and thus the delta function cannot be a physical contribution to the Berry curvature. We show this in two ways. First we observe that
\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{i}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{mm}=\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}\log\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})
is a total divergence so
if
the wavefunction could be continuously defined, then the Berry curvature would integrate to zero over the BZ. However, a Chern number forbids a globally well-defined gauge, which in our case is manifested by the undefined states where
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{*})=0
. To compute the Chern number, we define the wavefunction locally in patches. Let patch 1 be
BZ
with a small neighborhood near
\mathbf{k}^{*}
removed in the gauge where
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k}^{*})=0
, and let patch 2 be the small neighborhood around
\mathbf{k}^{*}
in a gauge where
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{p}^{*})=0
for some
\mathbf{p}^{*}
outside the neighborhood. The Berry curvature is non-singular in both patches and integrates to give
C=-1
. A more physical way to understand this comes from computing the Wilson loop. Because the Berry connection appears in the exponential, the
2\pi
delta function is unobservable as we now show. The Abelian Wilson loop of a single Landau level band integrated along the
k_{2}
direction is
Alexandradinata:2012sp

\displaystyle W(k_{1})=e^{i\theta_{B}(k_{1})}=\exp\left(i\oint_{(k_{1},0)}^{(k_{1},2\pi)}dk_{2}\mathcal{A}^{mm}_{2}(\mathbf{k})\right)

(254)

where
\theta_{B}(k_{1})
is the Berry phase whose winding determines the Chern number. We will calculate an exact expression for
W(k_{1})
. To start, it is easy to show that
\theta_{B}(0)=0
for the Wilson loop integrated along the
k_{2}
direction because
dk_{2}\mathcal{A}^{mm}_{2}(0,k_{2})=\frac{1}{2}dk_{2}\partial_{1}\log\mathcal{N}(0,k_{2})=0
, which follows from the fact that
\mathcal{N}(k_{1},k_{2})=\mathcal{N}(-k_{1},k_{2})
so
\partial_{1}\mathcal{N}|_{k_{1}=0}=0
. This is easily observed from Eq. (
138
) which shows
\mathcal{N}(k_{1},k_{2})
is even in both its arguments. We can now calculate directly

\displaystyle\theta_{B}(k_{1})=\theta_{B}(k_{1})-\theta_{B}(0)

\displaystyle=\left(\oint_{(k_{1},0)}^{(k_{1},2\pi)}-\oint_{(0)}^{(0,2\pi)}\right)dk_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{mm}(\mathbf{k})\mod 2\pi

(255)

\displaystyle=\int_{(0,k_{1})\times(0,2\pi)}d^{2}k\,\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{i}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{mm}(\mathbf{k})\mod 2\pi

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\pi k_{1}}{2\pi}+2\pi\Theta(k_{1}-\pi)\mod 2\pi

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-k_{1},&0\leq k_{1}<\pi\\
2\pi-k_{1},&\pi<k_{1}\leq 2\pi\\
\end{cases}

where the second equality follows from adding paths in opposite direction separated by
2\pi
which cancel, and then applying Stokes’ theorem (see Fig.
11
(a)). Finally
\Theta(x)
is the Heavyside step function:
\partial_{x}\Theta(x)=\delta(x)
. The winding of
\theta_{B}(k_{1})
is plotted in Fig.
11
(b). We see that the
2\pi
discontinuity from the delta function in Eq. (
253
) is unobservable because
\theta_{B}(k_{1})
is only defined mod
2\pi
.

Figure 11:
Landau Level Wilson Loops.
(a)
The Berry phase can be calculated using Stokes’s theorem in Eq. (
255
). The red arrows represent Wilson loops integrated along
k_{2}
, and the blue arrows represent two additional paths (which sum to zero because
k_{2}=0
and
k_{2}=2\pi
are identified) which form a closed loop. There is an additional delta function marked by the X at
(k_{1},k_{2})=(\pi,\pi)
which adds
2\pi
to the Berry phase as
k_{1}
crosses
\pi
.
(b)
We plot the Berry phase obtained from the Abelian Wilson loop in Eq. (
255
), from which we see that the winding gives
C=-1
.

Having discussed the Abelian terms from the Landau level basis states, we now consider the full non-Abelian Wilson loop with contributions from the band eigenvectors. We gather the terms from Eq. (
239
) and Eq. (
248
). Incorporating the prefactors, we find

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|\mathbf{b}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{r}|\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta_{mn}(-i\partial_{i})\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})

(256)

\displaystyle-(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\epsilon_{ij}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\delta_{mn}\partial_{j}\log\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})+[\tilde{Z}_{j}]_{mn}\Big)\ .

The explicit matrix elements are

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\tilde{Z}_{j}]_{mn}=\braket{m|\frac{z_{j}a^{\dagger}+\bar{z}_{j}a}{\sqrt{2\phi}}|n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\phi}}(z_{j}\sqrt{m}\delta_{m-1,n}+\bar{z}_{j}\sqrt{n}\delta_{m,n-1})\ .

(257)

Finally, we obtain an explicit expression for the non-Abelian Berry connection
\mathcal{A}^{MN}(\mathbf{k})
in the occupied bands indexed by
M,N
defined by

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\mathcal{A}^{MN}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{mn}[U^{\dagger}]^{M}_{m}\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|\mathbf{r}|\mathbf{k},n}U_{n}^{N}(\mathbf{k})

(258)

where
U(\mathbf{k})
is the
N_{LL}\times N_{occ}
matrix of eigenvectors,
N_{occ}
is the number of occupied bands, and
N_{LL}
is the dimension of the matrix Hamiltonian after the Landau levels are truncated above some cutoff. Plugging in Eq. (
248
), we find the final expression

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{A}_{i}^{MN}(\mathbf{k})={\bf{b}}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{A}^{MN}(\mathbf{k})=[U^{\dagger}(i\partial_{i}-\epsilon_{ij}\tilde{Z}_{j})U]^{MN}-\frac{\delta^{MN}}{2}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{j}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}

(259)

where we performed an identical manipulation to Eq. (
251
) to act the
\partial_{i}
derivative on
U(\mathbf{k})
.

To compute Wilson loops numerically, we need to be able to write the Wilson loop as a discretized product. To do so, we need to deal with the
\tilde{Z}_{j}
term using the commutation relations in Eq. (
227
). We need the following identity

\displaystyle e^{-i\epsilon_{jl}k_{j}\tilde{Z}_{l}}\partial_{i}e^{i\epsilon_{jl}k_{j}\tilde{Z}_{l}}

\displaystyle=i\epsilon_{il}\tilde{Z}_{l}-\frac{1}{2}[i\epsilon_{jl}k_{j}\tilde{Z}_{l},\partial_{i}(i\epsilon_{j^{\prime}l^{\prime}}k_{j^{\prime}}\tilde{Z}_{l^{\prime}})]+\text{higher commutators}

(260)

\displaystyle=i\epsilon_{il}\tilde{Z}_{l}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{jl}\epsilon_{il^{\prime}}k_{j}[\tilde{Z}_{l},\tilde{Z}_{l^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=i\epsilon_{ij}\tilde{Z}_{j}+\frac{i}{2\phi}\epsilon_{ll^{\prime}}\epsilon_{jl}\epsilon_{il^{\prime}}k_{j}

\displaystyle=i\epsilon_{ij}\tilde{Z}_{j}-\frac{i}{2\phi}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}

where the first line is the formula for the derivative of the exponential map (see Wikipedia for instance
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derivative_of_the_exponential_map
) and the higher commutator terms vanish because
[\tilde{Z}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{j}]\propto\mathbb{1}
is central. The additional
-\frac{i}{2\phi}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}
term has nonzero constant curl and so cannot be absorbed into the exponential. However,
-\frac{i}{2\phi}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}
is proportional to the identity so it can be factored out. Using Eq. (
260
), we have

\displaystyle U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})e^{-i\epsilon_{jl}k_{j}\tilde{Z}_{l}}i\partial_{i}\left(e^{i\epsilon_{jl}k_{j}\tilde{Z}_{l}}U(\mathbf{k})\right)

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\left(e^{-i\epsilon_{jl}k_{j}\tilde{Z}_{l}}i\partial_{i}e^{i\epsilon_{jl}k_{j}\tilde{Z}_{l}}\right)U(\mathbf{k})+U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})i\partial_{i}U(\mathbf{k})

(261)

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})i\partial_{i}U(\mathbf{k})+U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\left(-\epsilon_{ij}\tilde{Z}_{j}+\frac{1}{2\phi}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}\right)U(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\left(i\partial_{i}-\epsilon_{ij}\tilde{Z}_{j}\right)U(\mathbf{k})+\frac{1}{2\phi}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})U(\mathbf{k})

and because
U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})U(\mathbf{k})=\mathbb{1}
due to the orthonormality of the eigenvectors, we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{MN}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=[\tilde{U}^{\dagger}i\partial_{i}\tilde{U}]^{MN}-\frac{\delta^{MN}}{2\phi}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}-\frac{\delta^{MN}}{2}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{j}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right),\qquad\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k})\equiv e^{i\epsilon_{ij}k_{i}{\tilde{Z}}_{j}}U(\mathbf{k})=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}}U(\mathbf{k})

(262)

and we used
e^{i\epsilon_{ij}k_{i}\tilde{Z}_{j}}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}}
which is a unitary matrix. As such, note that
\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k})=U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}){\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}}U(\mathbf{k})=U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})U(\mathbf{k})=\mathbb{1}
. The exponential factor
e^{i\epsilon_{ij}k_{i}\tilde{Z}_{j}}
has nontrivial commutation relations and cannot be absorbed into the definition of
U({\bf{k}})
via a gauge transformation; we will keep this term throughout our calculations. The first term in Eq. (
262
) is in the canonical Berry connection form. The second term is the extraneous Abelian term (which we will show is canceled at the end of the calculation), and the third term of Eq. (
262
) is the winding of the Chern basis. As with the standard zero-flux Wilson loop, we want to express the Wilson loop as a product of projectors. In constructing the projector product, we will find that a term appears to exactly cancel the
-\frac{\delta^{MN}}{2\phi}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}
extraneous term. The end result will be a simple product formula in terms of
U(\mathbf{k})
. We define the path-ordered non-Abelian Wilson loop along the path
\mathcal{C}
to be
Alexandradinata:2012sp

\displaystyle\hbox{}[W_{\mathcal{C}}]^{MN}

\displaystyle=\left[\exp\left(i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{k})\right)\right]^{MN}

(263)

We now plug in Eq. (
262
) and factor out the Abelian contributions:

\displaystyle W_{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\times\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}\left[e^{-\frac{i}{2\phi}\int_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}}\exp\left(i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}\,\tilde{U}^{\dagger}i\partial_{i}\tilde{U}\right)\right]\ .

(264)

Note that we have grouped the extraneous Abelian term with the
\tilde{U}
term inside the brackets. Our goal is now to produce a discretized expression for the bracketed term. To do so, we break the path
\mathcal{C}
into
L
increments labeled by
d\mathbf{k}_{n},n=1,\dots L
where
d\mathbf{k}_{n}=\mathbf{k}_{n}-\mathbf{k}_{n-1}
. Because
\mathcal{C}
is a closed path,
\mathbf{k}_{0}\equiv\mathbf{k}_{L}
. Taking
L\to\infty
, we find

\displaystyle\exp\left(i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}\,\tilde{U}^{\dagger}i\partial_{i}\tilde{U}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(i\sum_{n=1}^{L}\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})i(\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n})-\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n-1}))\right)

(265)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(i\sum_{n=1}^{L}i(\mathbb{1}-\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n-1}))\right)

and now using BCH (neglecting terms of
O(d\mathbf{k}^{2})
which vanish as
L\to\infty
), we have

\displaystyle\exp\left(i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}\,\tilde{U}^{\dagger}i\partial_{i}\tilde{U}\right)

\displaystyle\to\prod_{n}^{L\leftarrow 1}\exp\left((-\mathbb{1}+\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n-1}))\right)

(266)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n}^{L\leftarrow 1}\left(\mathbb{1}+(-\mathbb{1}+\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n-1}))+\dots\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n}^{L\leftarrow 1}\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n-1})

where in the second to last line, we expanded
e^{M}=1+M+\dots
where
M=\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n-1})-\mathbb{1}
is a matrix of order
O(d\mathbf{k})
. Terms of higher order vanish in the
L\to\infty
limit. So far, this argument is identical to the standard Wilson loop. Regrouping terms in the product gives the standard Wilson loop form

\displaystyle\exp\left(i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}\,\tilde{U}^{\dagger}i\partial_{i}\tilde{U}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{L})\left(\prod_{n}^{L-1\leftarrow 1}\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\right)\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{0})\ .

(267)

The factors in parentheses are a product of
\tilde{U}
projectors. By plugging in
\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k})=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}}U(\mathbf{k})
, we will arrive at an expression written only in terms of the matrix eigenvectors
U(\mathbf{k})
:

\displaystyle\prod_{n}^{L-1\leftarrow 1}\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{L-1}}P(\mathbf{k}_{L-1})\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{L-1}}\dots\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{2}}P(\mathbf{k}_{2})\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{1}}P(\mathbf{k}_{1})\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{1}},\quad P(\mathbf{k})\equiv U(\mathbf{k})U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(268)

To simplify the Wilson loop further, we focus on factors in the form
\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{n+1}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{n}}
. To do so, recall that
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}}=e^{i\epsilon_{ij}k_{i}\tilde{Z}_{j}}
and use the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff identity
e^{X+Y}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]}e^{X}e^{Y}
for
[X,Y]
a
c
-number to show

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{n+1}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{n}}=\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}-\mathbf{k}_{n}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{n}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}[-i\epsilon_{ij}dk^{n+1}_{i}\tilde{Z}_{j},-i\epsilon_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}k^{n}_{i^{\prime}}\tilde{Z}_{j^{\prime}}]\right)\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}}\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{n}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{n}}

(269)

where we raised the
n
indices on
d\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{k}
to avoid confusion with the vector indices
i,j
. The commutator is direct to evaluate with Eq. (
227
):

\displaystyle\hbox{}[-i\epsilon_{ij}dk^{n+1}_{i}\tilde{Z}_{j},-i\epsilon_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}k^{n}_{i^{\prime}}\tilde{Z}_{j^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=-\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}dk^{n+1}_{i}k^{n}_{i^{\prime}}[\tilde{Z}_{j},\tilde{Z}_{j^{\prime}}]

(270)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}dk^{n+1}_{i}k^{n}_{i^{\prime}}\frac{i}{\phi}\epsilon_{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\phi}\epsilon_{ij}dk^{n+1}_{i}k^{n}_{j}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\phi}d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}\times\mathbf{k}_{n}\ .

Returning to Eq. (
269
), we now have the simple relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{n+1}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{n}}

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{i}{2\phi}d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}\times\mathbf{k}_{n}}\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}}

(271)

using
\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{n}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{n}}=\mathbb{1}
by unitarity. With this result, Eq. (
268
) reads

\displaystyle\prod_{n}^{L-1\leftarrow 1}\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{i}{2\phi}\sum_{n=1}^{L-2}d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}\times\mathbf{k}_{n}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{k}_{L-1}}P(\mathbf{k}_{L-1})\dots\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{3}}P(\mathbf{k}_{2})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{2}}P(\mathbf{k}_{1})\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{k}_{1}}

(272)

\displaystyle\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{L})\left(\prod_{n}^{L-1\leftarrow 1}\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{n})\right)\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k}_{n})

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{i}{2\phi}\sum_{n=0}^{L-1}d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}\times\mathbf{k}_{n}}U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{L})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{L}}P(\mathbf{k}_{L-1})\dots\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{3}}P(\mathbf{k}_{2})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{2}}P(\mathbf{k}_{1})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{1}}U(\mathbf{k}_{0})

where we grouped the Abelian
-\frac{i}{\phi}d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}\times\mathbf{k}_{n}
terms into an overall prefactor. Now we have a simple expression for the
\tilde{U}
Wilson loop in Eq. (
267
) which reads

\displaystyle\exp\left(i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}\,\tilde{U}^{\dagger}i\partial_{i}\tilde{U}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{i}{2\phi}\sum_{n=0}^{L-1}d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}\times\mathbf{k}_{n}}U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{L})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{L}}\left(\prod_{n}^{L-1\leftarrow 1}P(\mathbf{k}_{n})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{n}}\right)U(\mathbf{k}_{0})\ .

(273)

Because we have taken
L\to\infty
, the Abelian overall phase becomes

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{i}{2\phi}\sum_{n=0}^{L-1}d\mathbf{k}_{n+1}\times\mathbf{k}_{n}=\frac{i}{2\phi}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}\ .

(274)

This prefactor exactly cancels the extraneous Abelian term in Eq. (
264
), which now reads

\displaystyle\boxed{W_{\mathcal{C}}=e^{-i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\times\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\log\vartheta(\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}|\Phi)}U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}_{L})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{L}}P(\mathbf{k}_{L-1})\dots P(\mathbf{k}_{2})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{2}}P(\mathbf{k}_{1})\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{1}}U(\mathbf{k}_{0})}

(275)

showing that the Wilson loop at
\phi=2\pi
factors into an Abelian winding factor that gives each Landau level a nonzero Chern number, and a non-Abelian product of projectors with insertions of
\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}}
. To understand the effect of the insertions, we consider the Berry connection (Eq. (
259
)) when
all
bands are occupied, so
U(\mathbf{k})=\mathbb{1}
. Because
U(\mathbf{k})
is independent of
\mathbf{k}
in this case, the
\mathbf{k}
derivative term disappears and

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{MN}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=[(-\epsilon_{ij}\tilde{Z}_{j})]^{MN}-\frac{\delta^{MN}}{2}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{j}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\ .

(276)

In a crystal at zero flux, the Berry connection of fully occupied bands is identically zero, but we see from the above expression that two contributions survive at
\phi=2\pi
. Using Eq. (
257
) to show that
\tilde{Z}_{j}
is traceless, we see that only the Abelian term (the second term in Eq. (
276
)) contributes to the Berry curvature and therefore the winding. However, the non-Abelian term is nontrivial. We compute the Wilson loop along a straight contour
\mathcal{C}=(k_{1},0)\to(k_{1},2\pi)
, finding:

\displaystyle W_{\mathcal{C}}=\exp\left(i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}(\mathbf{k})\right)

\displaystyle=e^{i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dk_{i}(-\epsilon_{ij}\tilde{Z}_{j})}e^{-i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\times\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}

(277)

\displaystyle=e^{-i(2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2})_{i}\epsilon_{ij}\tilde{Z}_{j}}e^{-i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\times\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}e^{-i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\times\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}

showing that
\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}
can be interpreted as the non-Abelian factor of the Wilson loop in the fully occupied limit (note that it does not depend on
k_{1}
so does not contribute to the winding). Importantly, this result agrees with Eq. (
275
) which in the
U(\mathbf{k})=\mathbb{1}
limit reads

\displaystyle W_{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\times\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{L}}\left(\prod_{n}^{L-1\leftarrow 1}\mathcal{H}^{-d\mathbf{k}_{n}}\right)

(278)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\times\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}\prod_{n}^{L\leftarrow 1}\mathcal{H}^{-\frac{2\pi}{L}\mathbf{b}_{2}}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\oint_{\mathcal{C}}d\mathbf{k}\times\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\log\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}

using Eq. (
226
) in the last line. The physical picture of Eq. (
277
) and Eq. (
278
) is that, unlike the atomic limits of zero-flux crystals, the fully occupied Landau level state has a nontrivial Wilson loop where every band winds identically due to the
\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{j}\log\vartheta
term. The
k_{1}
-independent
\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}
term can be diagonalized to obtain the Wannier centers which are the Wilson loop eigenvalues
Alexandradinata:2012sp
;
Marzari et al. 2012
.

Appendix B
Square Lattice Calculations

This section is brief, pedagogically oriented review of how Bloch’s theorem is used to produce a matrix Hamiltonian on a plane wave basis at zero flux (App.
B.1
) and how our magnetic Bloch theorem is used to produce a matrix Hamiltonian on a Landau level basis at
2\pi
flux (App.
B.2
). We use a simple
p^{2}/(2m)
kinetic Hamiltonian in a cosine potential on the square lattice.

B.1
Zero Flux

The zero-flux Hamiltonian is chosen to be

\displaystyle H^{\phi=0}({\bf{r}})=-\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}^{2}+\frac{w}{2}(e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{1}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{2}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+H.c.)

(279)

If
w=0
, the solution to the Hamiltonian is simple – the eigenstates are plane waves of the form

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{\tilde{\bf{k}}}({\bf{r}})=e^{i{\tilde{\bf{k}}}\cdot{\bf{r}}},

(280)

where
{\tilde{\bf{k}}}
runs over the entire plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. When the lattice potential is added, the continuous translation symmetry is broken down to a discrete symmetry indexed by
{\bf{k}}
, where
{\bf{k}}
is defined in first Brillouin zone
{\bf{k}}=k_{1}{\bf{b}}_{1}+k_{2}{\bf{b}}_{2},k_{1,2}\in(-\pi,\pi]

. States at
\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G}
where
{\bf{G}}=2\pi G_{1}{\bf{b}}_{1}+2\pi G_{2}{\bf{b}}_{2},G_{1,2}\in\mathbb{Z}
can be scattered to states at
\mathbf{k}
by the periodic potential. The states

\displaystyle\psi_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}({\bf{r}})=\tilde{\psi}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{G}}}({\bf{r}}).

(281)

form a basis of the Hilbert space on which we can find a representation of the Hamiltonian, which will necessarily be diagonal in
\mathbf{k}
. This selection rule arises because
\psi_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}({\bf{r}})
is an eigenstate of the translation operator:
\psi_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}({\bf{r}}+\mathbf{a}_{i})=e^{i(\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}}\psi_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}({\bf{r}})=e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}}\psi_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}({\bf{r}})
using the fact that
\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{i}\in\mathds{Z}
. For each crystal momentum
{\bf{k}}
in the first BZ, the plane wave index
{\bf{G}}
labels different states with the same crystal momentum.

We calculate the matrix elements of the Hamiltonian in the
\ket{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}
basis, where
\langle{\bf{r}}|{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}\rangle=\psi_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}({\bf{r}})
. Working in the
{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}
basis, the Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle\bra{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}H^{\phi=0}\ket{{\bf{k}}^{\prime},{\bf{G}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}rd^{2}r^{\prime}\,\braket{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}|{\bf{r}}}\bra{{\bf{r}}}H^{\phi=0}\ket{{\bf{r}}^{\prime}}\braket{{\bf{r}}^{\prime}|{\bf{k}}^{\prime},{\bf{G}}^{\prime}}

(282)

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}rd^{2}r^{\prime}\,\psi^{*}_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}({\bf{r}})H^{\phi=0}({\bf{r}})\delta({\bf{r}}-{\bf{r}}^{\prime})\psi_{{\bf{k}}^{\prime},{\bf{G}}^{\prime}}({\bf{r}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i({\bf{k}}-{\bf{G}})\cdot{\bf{r}}}H^{\phi=0}({\bf{r}})e^{i({\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{G}}^{\prime})\cdot{\bf{r}}}

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i({\bf{k}}-{\bf{G}})\cdot{\bf{r}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}({\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{G}}^{\prime})^{2}+\frac{w}{2}(e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{1}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{2}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+H.c.)\right]e^{i({\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{G}}^{\prime})\cdot{\bf{r}}}

The kinetic term has no position dependence, so

\displaystyle\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i({\bf{k}}-{\bf{G}})\cdot{\bf{r}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}({\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{G}}^{\prime})^{2}\right]e^{i({\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{G}}^{\prime})\cdot{\bf{r}}}=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\left[\frac{1}{2}({\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{G}}^{\prime})^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}\right]

(283)

which is diagonal in
\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{G}
. The potential term does have position dependence and will not be diagonal in
\mathbf{G}
:

\displaystyle\frac{w}{2}\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i({\bf{k}}-{\bf{G}})\cdot{\bf{r}}}\left[e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{1}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{2}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+H.c.\right]e^{i({\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{G}}^{\prime})\cdot{\bf{r}}}

(284)

\displaystyle=\frac{w}{2}\int d^{2}r\,\left[e^{-i({\bf{k}}-{\bf{G}}-{\bf{k}}^{\prime}+{\bf{G}}^{\prime}-2\pi{\bf{b}}_{1})\cdot{\bf{r}}}+e^{-i({\bf{k}}-{\bf{G}}-{\bf{k}}^{\prime}+{\bf{G}}^{\prime}-2\pi{\bf{b}}_{2})\cdot{\bf{r}}}+H.c.\right]

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\frac{w}{2}\left[\delta_{\mathbf{G}+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{G}}+\delta_{\mathbf{G}+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2},\mathbf{G}}+\delta_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2},\mathbf{G}}\right]\ .

This term is still diagonal in
\mathbf{k}
as ensures by the translation symmetry. We derive the Bloch Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\bra{{\bf{k}},{\bf{G}}}H^{\phi=0}\ket{{\bf{k}}^{\prime},{\bf{G}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})H^{\phi=0}_{\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}),

(285)

\displaystyle H^{\phi=0}_{\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}({\bf{k}}^{\prime}-{\bf{G}}^{\prime})^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}+\frac{w}{2}(\delta_{\mathbf{G}+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}+\delta_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1},\mathbf{G}}+\delta_{\mathbf{G}+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2},\mathbf{G}}+\delta_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2},\mathbf{G}})\ .

The Bloch Hamiltonian can be thought of as an infinite matrix on the
\mathbf{G}
basis as a function of
\mathbf{k}
. By imposing a cutoff on the
\mathbf{G}
plane wave states, this matrix can be diagonalized and the spectrum obtained.

B.2
2\pi
-flux

The Hamiltonian at
2\pi
flux is created via canonical substitution, sending
-i\boldsymbol{\nabla}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\pi}
:

\displaystyle H^{\phi=2\pi}({\bf{r}})=\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{2}+\frac{w}{2}(e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{1}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{2}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+H.c.).

(286)

At
2\pi
flux, the magnetic translation operators commute with
H^{\phi=2\pi}({\bf{r}})
and allow us to represent
H^{\phi=2\pi}({\bf{r}})
as a matrix acting on the
\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
eigenstates of the magnetic translation operators. We see that the Landau levels play the role of the plane waves at zero flux. The substantial difference is that, instead of simple Bloch states, the magnetic translation eigenstates are given in Eq. (
94
).

The matrix elements in the magnetic translation basis read

\displaystyle\bra{{\bf{k}},m}H^{\phi=2\pi}({\bf{r}})\ket{{\bf{k}}^{\prime},n}

\displaystyle=\bra{{\bf{k}},m}\phi(a^{\dagger}a+\frac{1}{2})+\frac{w}{2}(e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{1}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+e^{-2\pi i{\bf{b}}_{2}\cdot{\bf{r}}}+H.c.)\ket{{\bf{k}}^{\prime},n}

(287)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{2}\delta({\bf{k}}-{\bf{k}}^{\prime})\left[\phi(m+\frac{1}{2})\delta_{mn}+\frac{w}{2}(e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}_{mn}+e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}_{mn}+H.c.)\right]

where we used
\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{2}=\phi(a^{\dagger}a+\frac{1}{2})
which acts diagonal on the
m,n
Landau level indices because
a^{\dagger}a\ket{n}=n\ket{n}
. The matrix elements of the potential term were computed in App.
A.6
, and an expression for
\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{G}}
may be found in Eq. (
223
). Thus the magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian
H^{\phi=2\pi}_{mn}({\bf{k}})
is defined

\displaystyle\bra{{\bf{k}},m}H^{\phi=2\pi}({\bf{r}})\ket{{\bf{k}}^{\prime},n}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})H^{\phi=2\pi}_{mn}({\bf{k}})\ .

(288)

In analogy to the zero flux case,
H^{\phi=2\pi}_{mn}({\bf{k}})
is a matrix on the
mn
Landau level indices as a function of
\mathbf{k}
, and can be diagonalized by imposing a Landau level cutoff. See Fig.
12
for a comparison for the density of states obtained by our band structures versus the open momentum space method in Ref.
Lian et al. 2021a
.

Figure 12:
Density of states computed from the band structure (blue) versus the open momentum space method (orange) developed in Ref.
Lian et al. 2021a
. The small discrepancies can be improved by sampling more points in both methods.

Appendix C
Coulomb Interaction in a Gauge-Invariant Formalism

In this Appendix, we show how to write the Coulomb interaction

\displaystyle H_{int}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{2}rd^{2}r^{\prime}\,n(\mathbf{r})V(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})n(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,V(\mathbf{q})\rho_{-\mathbf{q}}\rho_{\mathbf{q}},

(289)

in the terms of the magnetic translation group eigenstates in a gauge-invariant manner. We keep
V(\mathbf{q})
general but require the Fourier transform is even,
V(\mathbf{q})=V(-\mathbf{q})
, and is non-negative real,
V(\mathbf{q})\geq 0
, to preserve the positive semi-definite structure. We require a formula for the Fourier components of the density

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbf{q}}=\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}n(\mathbf{r})

(290)

written in terms of the magnetic translation group eigenstates. The single-particle magnetic translation group eigenstates are

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1}}T_{\mathbf{a}_{2}}^{\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2}}\frac{a^{{\dagger}n}}{\sqrt{n!}}\psi_{0},\qquad a\psi_{0}=b\psi_{0}=0\ .

(291)

The single-particle eigenstates arises from diagonalizing the single-particle Hamiltonian on this basis, yielding matrix eigenvectors
U^{N}_{n}(\mathbf{k})
where
N
is the band index and
n
is the Landau level orbital index. The continuum electron operators obey
\{c^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r}),c(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})\}=\delta(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})
, and the creation operators for the magnetic translation group basis states are denoted
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},N}=\sum_{n}U^{N}_{n}(\mathbf{k})\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}
. The magnetic translation group eigenstates obey

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{r}|\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}|0}=\braket{0|c(\mathbf{r})\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}|0}=\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}(\mathbf{r})\ .

(292)

Our goal now is to express the density operator
n(\mathbf{r})=c^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})c(\mathbf{r})
in terms of the single-particle eigenstates. If there are any internal indices of
c
, like spin or valley in TBG, they are implicitly summed over:
n(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})c_{\alpha}(\mathbf{r})
. Because
\rho(\mathbf{q})
is bilinear in fermion operators, we only need to calculate its single-particle matrix elements. Inserting resolutions of the identity and using
\braket{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}|n(\mathbf{r})|\mathbf{r}^{\prime\prime}}=\delta(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime\prime})
, we find

\displaystyle\braket{0|\psi_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}\rho_{\mathbf{q}}\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}|0}

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i{\bf{q}}\cdot{\bf{r}}}\braket{0|\psi_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}c^{\dagger}({\bf{r}})c({\bf{r}})\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}|0}

(293)

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i{\bf{q}}\cdot{\bf{r}}}\braket{0|\psi_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}c^{\dagger}({\bf{r}})|0}\braket{0|c({\bf{r}})\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}|0}

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i{\bf{q}}\cdot{\bf{r}}}\psi^{*}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}({\bf{r}})\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}({\bf{r}})

\displaystyle=\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}\ .

where in the second line we have inserted a resolution of identity, but because of fermion conservation only the vacuum survives. Hence we obtain an explicit expression for

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{mn}\int\frac{d^{2}kd^{2}k^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}

(294)

where the matrix element
\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}
was computed in Eq. (
235
). This is essentially the density operator
\rho_{\bf{q}}
in the Landau level basis. Thus we have determined the coefficients in the expansion of
\rho_{\mathbf{q}}
:

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{mn}\int\frac{d^{2}kd^{2}k^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\braket{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m|e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}|\mathbf{k},n}\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}=\sum_{mn}\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},m}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}_{mn}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}\ .

(295)

For convenience, we recall that

\displaystyle e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{4\phi}}\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}{\sqrt{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}}

(296)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}_{mn}

\displaystyle=\bra{m}\exp\left(i\frac{\gamma_{q}a+\bar{\gamma}_{q}a^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)\ket{n}=e^{-\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{q}\gamma_{q}}{4\phi}}\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{m!}{n!}}\left(\frac{i\gamma_{q}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{n-m}L_{m}^{|n-m|}\left(\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{q}\gamma_{q}}{2\phi}\right),&n>m\\
\sqrt{\frac{n!}{m!}}\left(\frac{i\bar{\gamma}_{q}}{\sqrt{2\phi}}\right)^{m-n}L_{n}^{|m-n|}\left(\frac{\bar{\gamma}_{q}\gamma_{q}}{2\phi}\right),&m>n\\
\end{cases}

which are both unitary. It is simple now to write
\rho_{\mathbf{q}}
in terms of the single-particle eigenstate basis by recalling that in the
N
th band

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}U^{N}_{m}(\mathbf{k})\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m}\ .

(297)

The creation operator
\gamma_{{\bf{k}},N}^{\dagger}
has no relation to the momentum factor
\gamma_{q}=\epsilon_{ij}q_{i}\bar{z}_{j}
. If our Landau levels carried additional indices
\alpha
, the unitary
U
would also be a matrix in
\alpha,\beta
.

Orthogonality of the eigenvectors at a given
\mathbf{k}
gives
\sum_{N}U^{N*}_{n}(\mathbf{k})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},N}=\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}
and hence

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}\sum_{MN}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},M}[U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}U(\mathbf{k})]_{MN}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N}\ .

(298)

To project the Hamiltonian into a set of low energy bands, one merely restricts the sum over
MN
. Our final step is to define the form factor

\displaystyle M_{MN}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\equiv e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}[U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}U(\mathbf{k})]_{MN}\ .

(299)

A few comments about
M(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
are in order. For all
\mathbf{q}
, the eigenvalues of
M(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
have magnitudes less than or equal to
1
because
e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}
is unitary and
U(\mathbf{k})
is composed from normalized eigenvectors. At generic
\mathbf{q}
,
M(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
does not have a gauge-invariant spectrum because
U(\mathbf{k})
and
U(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})
are eigenvector matrices and each come with arbitrary phases. At
\mathbf{q}=2\pi\mathbf{G}
,
U(\mathbf{k})
and
U(\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G})
are identical because
\mathbf{k}
is periodic on the BZ. This is because the states
\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}(\mathbf{r})
are explicitly periodic in
\mathbf{k}
, so Hamiltonian is explicitly translation-invariant. Thus
M(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
has a gauge-invariant spectrum. This eigenvector gauge-invariance is important because later expressions will depend on the spectrum of
M(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
. Using
\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{q}}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}\,{\dagger}}
, we find

\displaystyle M(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})^{\dagger}=e^{-i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{q}}U(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})=e^{-i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})-i\xi_{-\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})}M(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},-\mathbf{q})=M(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},-\mathbf{q})

(300)

where we used the theta function identity
e^{-i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})-i\xi_{-\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})}=1
. This is proved by observing

\displaystyle e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})+i\xi_{-\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{4\phi}}\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}{\sqrt{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}}\frac{e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{4\phi}}\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1}+q/2,k_{2}-q_{2}-iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}{\sqrt{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}}

(301)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{2\phi}}\frac{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-\bar{q}/2,k_{2}-i\bar{q}/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\bar{q}q}{2\phi}}\frac{\left|\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q/2,k_{2}+iq/2)}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\right|^{2}}{\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1},k_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)\vartheta\left(\left.\frac{(k_{1}-q_{1},k_{2}-q_{2})}{2\pi}\right|\Phi\right)}>0

where we have used Eq. (
117
) which states
\theta(\mathbf{z}^{*}|\Phi)
=
\theta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)^{*}
and that
\theta(\mathbf{z}|\Phi)
is real and positive for
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Because
|e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})+i\xi_{-\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})}|=1
and
e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})+i\xi_{-\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})}
is real and positive, we find
e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})+i\xi_{-\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})}=1
. Eq. (
300
) is the Hermiticity of form factor, as described in Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021c
in zero flux where it is also true that
M^{\dagger}({\bf{k}},{\bf{q}})=M({\bf{k}}-{\bf{q}},-{\bf{q}})
.

Finally, we remark that
M(\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G},\mathbf{q})=M(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
because
U(\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G})=U(\mathbf{k})
and
e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G})}=e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}
as follows from the periodicity of the Siegel theta functions in
\mathbf{k}
. However,
M(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
is not periodic in
\mathbf{q}
, which is expected because
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a continuum momentum.
As a function of
\mathbf{q}
at fixed
mn
,
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}_{mn}
is the product of a decaying exponential factor in
\gamma_{\mathbf{q}}\bar{\gamma}_{\mathbf{q}}\sim|\mathbf{q}|^{2}
and a polynomial factor in
\gamma_{\mathbf{q}}
. Thus for large
\mathbf{q}
, this term will decay exponentially but with subleading power law growth.

We end this section with the explicit form of the Coulomb interaction Hamiltonian. The density operator is written in terms of the form factor via

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=\int_{BZ}\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{MN}M_{MN}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},M}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N}

(302)

which leads to the expression

\displaystyle H_{int}=\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,V(\mathbf{q})\rho_{-\mathbf{q}}\rho_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,O_{-\mathbf{q}}O_{\mathbf{q}},\qquad O_{\mathbf{q}}=\sqrt{V(\mathbf{q})}\int_{BZ}\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{MN}M_{MN}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},M}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N}

(303)

which is positive semi-definite because
O_{-\mathbf{q}}=O_{\mathbf{q}}^{\dagger}
as follows from Eq. (
300
). For numerical calculations, it is convenient to discretize the momentum integrals into a sum over
L^{2}
terms according to

\displaystyle\int_{BZ}\frac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}f(\mathbf{k})\to\frac{1}{\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}f(\mathbf{k}),\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}f(\mathbf{q})\to\frac{1}{\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}f(\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G})

(304)

where
\Omega_{tot}=L^{2}\Omega
is the total area of an
L\mathbf{a}_{1}\times L\mathbf{a}_{2}
sample, and there are
L^{2}
terms in the BZ sums, and the sum
\mathbf{G}
is over the reciprocal lattice vectors
\mathbf{G}=\mathds{Z}\mathbf{b}_{1}+\mathds{Z}\mathbf{b}_{2}
. Notice that in Eq. (
304
), the
{\bf{k}}
sum goes over the BZ. The
\mathbf{q}
integral is over all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. It is useful to write
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G}
where
\mathbf{k}
is integrated over the BZ and
\mathbf{G}
is summed over the reciprocal lattice. Eq. (
304
) follows by approximating the
BZ
integral as a sum.

Appendix D
Bistritzer-MacDonald Hamiltonian at
2\pi
Flux

In this Appendix, we discuss the Bistritzer-MacDonald (BM) model of TBG which is the central physical motivation for this work. In App.
D.1
, we lay out our conventions for the moiré unit cell, the Bistritzer-MacDonald (BM) Hamiltonian, and the explicit form of the single-particle Hamiltonian at
2\pi
flux. We then discuss the symmetries of the full two-valley system which are relevant for the many-body calculations in App.
D.2
. A discussion of the strong coupling expansion used to treat the Coulomb interaction is given in App.
D.3
. App.
D.4
contains a derivation of the exact eigenstates at even integer fillings.

D.1
Moiré Lattice Conventions and Single-particle Hamiltonian

Let us set our conventions for the geometry of the moiré twist unit cell. First, the graphene unit cell has a lattice vector of length
a_{g}=.246
nm and an area
\Omega_{g}=a_{g}^{2}\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
. The gaphene K point is
\mathbf{K}_{g}=\frac{2\pi}{a_{g}}(0,2/3)
. The moiré vectors are defined by the difference in momentum space of the rotated layers’
K
points:

\displaystyle 2\pi\mathbf{q}_{1}=(R_{\theta/2}-R_{-\theta/2})\mathbf{K}_{g},\quad\mathbf{q}_{j}=C_{3}\mathbf{q}_{j-1},\quad 2\pi|\mathbf{q}_{j}|=k_{\theta}=2\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\,|\mathbf{K}_{g}|=\frac{8\pi\sin\frac{\theta}{2}}{3a_{g}}\ .

(305)

Note that in this convention, we keep a factor of
2\pi
explicit. The moiré reciprocal lattice vectors are defined

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{j}=\mathbf{q}_{j}-\mathbf{q}_{3},\quad\mathbf{b}_{1}\times\mathbf{b}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\sin\frac{\theta}{2})^{2}}{\Omega_{g}},\qquad\mathbf{k}=k_{1}\mathbf{b}_{1}+k_{2}\mathbf{b}_{2},\ k_{i}\in(-\pi,\pi)\ .

(306)

Note that the moiré BZ has area
(2\pi)^{2}\mathbf{b}_{1}\times\mathbf{b}_{2}
which is smaller than the graphene BZ by a factor of
(2\sin\frac{\theta}{2})^{2}\sim\theta^{2}
. This is because the lattice constant of the moiré unit cell is larger by a factor of
\theta
. The moire lattice is defined by
