\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{j}=\delta_{ij}
which yields in cartesian coordinates

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{1}=\frac{a_{g}}{2\sin\frac{\theta}{2}}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right)\quad\mathbf{a}_{2}=\frac{a_{g}}{2\sin\frac{\theta}{2}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right),\qquad\Omega=\mathbf{a}_{1}\times\mathbf{a}_{2}=\frac{\Omega_{g}}{(2\sin\frac{\theta}{2})^{2}}\ .

(307)

In the range of the magic angle at
\theta=1.05^{\circ}
, the moiré unit cell is 3000 times larger than the graphene unit cell. The magnetic translation group is commutative when
\phi=\frac{eB\Omega}{\hbar}=2\pi
where we temporarily restore
\hbar=h/(2\pi)
. In physical units, the corresponding magnetic field is in the range

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{h}{e\Omega}=\frac{h}{e\Omega_{g}}\theta^{2}\in(25,32)\text{T for }\theta\in(1.03^{\circ},1.15^{\circ})\ .

(308)

The BM Hamiltonian at
\phi=2\pi
can be written (with
\sigma_{i}
and
T_{j}
acting on the sublattice indices)

\displaystyle H^{\phi}_{BM}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}&T(\mathbf{r})^{\dagger}\\
T(\mathbf{r})&v_{F}\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\\
\end{pmatrix},\qquad T(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{j=1}^{3}T_{j}e^{i2\pi\mathbf{q}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{r}},\quad T_{j+1}=\begin{pmatrix}w_{0}&w_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}j}\\
w_{1}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}j}&w_{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}\ .

(309)

In this gauge, Hamiltonian is not in Bloch form because the off-diagonals are do not have the lattice periodicity. This is because of the offset of the layer BZs. To remedy this, we perform a unitary transformation with the momentum shift matrix
\text{diag}(e^{i\pi\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}},e^{-i\pi\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}})
which puts the Hamiltonian in Bloch form

\displaystyle H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\pi v_{F}\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}&T_{1}+T_{2}e^{-i2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+T_{3}e^{-i2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}e^{i2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+T_{3}e^{i2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}}&v_{F}\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\pi v_{F}\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\\
\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathbf{b}_{j}=\mathbf{q}_{j+1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}\ .

(310)

We now need to compute the overlaps of this matrix with the magnetic Bloch irrep states. A suitable basis is
\ket{\mathbf{k},l,\alpha,n}\equiv\ket{l}\otimes\ket{\alpha}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
where
l=0,1
is the layer index and
\alpha=0,1
is the sublattice index. The Hamiltonian in this basis reads

\displaystyle H^{\phi=2\pi}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{2}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\end{pmatrix},\quad h(\boldsymbol{\pi})=\left(\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2\pi}\right)^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}0&a^{\dagger}\\
a&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

(311)

where
a,a^{\dagger},\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}
are all matrices in the Landau level basis. We compute the band structure of this matrix by truncating the Landau levels from
n=0,\dots,n_{LL}
. Because of the truncation, the highest Landau level will be annihilated by the kinetic term, and introduces a two spurious states at each
\mathbf{k}
(one per layer). These bands are easily removed.

D.2
Symmetries

In this section, we discuss the single-particle BM Hamiltonians in the
K
and
K^{\prime}
valleys and demonstrate the
U(1)
valley symmetry,
C_{2z}
, and
P
symmetries which survive at all flux and are essential for the Coulomb Hamiltonian in App.
D.3
. We also discuss the chiral symmetry
C
.

The BM Hamiltonian at zero flux reads

\displaystyle H_{K}^{\phi=0}=\begin{pmatrix}-iv_{F}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}&T({\bf{r}})^{\dagger}\\
T({\bf{r}})&-iv_{F}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\end{pmatrix},

(312)

where the
T(\mathbf{r})
moiré potentials are given in Eq. (
309
). In particular,
T_{1}=w_{0}\sigma_{0}+w_{1}\sigma_{1}
and
T_{j}=e^{\frac{2\pi i(j-1)}{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i(j-1)}{3}\sigma_{3}}
. The matrices
\sigma_{i}
are Pauli matrices acting on the graphene sublattice index. The layer index is often in matrix notation, as in Eq. (
312
), and we will use
\tau_{i}
Pauli matrices to denote the layer index as well. For many-body calculations, we must also study the Hamiltonian in the
K^{\prime}
valley, which is related to the
K
valley Hamiltonian at
\phi=0
by spinless time reversal
T
which acts as complex conjugation:

\displaystyle H_{K^{\prime}}^{\phi=0}=\begin{pmatrix}iv_{F}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}&T^{*}({\bf{r}})^{\dagger}\\
T^{*}({\bf{r}})&iv_{F}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\ .

(313)

The two Hamiltonians in Eq. (
312
) and Eq. (
313
) can be written as

\displaystyle H^{\phi=0}_{K}

\displaystyle=-iv_{F}\tau_{0}(\sigma_{1}\partial_{x}+\sigma_{2}\partial_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\tau_{1}\cos(2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})T_{j}+\tau_{2}\sin(2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})T_{j}

(314)

\displaystyle H^{\phi=0}_{K^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=iv_{F}\tau_{0}(\sigma_{1}\partial_{x}-\sigma_{2}\partial_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\tau_{1}\cos(2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})T_{j}^{*}-\tau_{2}\sin(2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})T_{j}^{*}.

We will use
\mu_{i}
as the Pauli matrices acting on the graphene valley degree of freedom while
\tau_{i}
are the Pauli matrices acting on the layer. In this notation, Eq. (
314
) can be written as one equation:

\displaystyle H^{\phi=0}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=-iv_{F}\tau_{0}(\mu_{3}\sigma_{1}\partial_{x}+\mu_{0}\sigma_{2}\partial_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mu_{0}\tau_{1}\cos 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}}+\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sin 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}

(315)

which is the full two-valley Hamiltonian. It is direct to check that time reversal is a symmetry of
H^{\phi=0}
(which it must be because
H^{\phi=0}
is simply the
K
valley with its time-reversed partner) by using the form
\mathcal{T}=\mu_{1}K,\mathcal{T}^{2}=+1
on the two-valley model:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}H^{\phi=0}(\mathbf{r})\mathcal{T}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}\left(iv_{F}\tau_{0}(\mu_{3}\sigma_{1}\partial_{x}-\mu_{0}\sigma_{2}\partial_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mu_{0}\tau_{1}\cos 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}}-\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sin 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}\right)\mu_{1}

(316)

\displaystyle=iv_{F}\tau_{0}(-\mu_{3}\sigma_{1}\partial_{x}-\mu_{0}\sigma_{2}\partial_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mu_{0}\tau_{1}\cos 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}}+\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sin 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})e^{(-1)^{2}\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}

\displaystyle=H^{\phi=0}(\mathbf{r})\ .

It is now a simple matter to obtain the real space Hamiltonian at nonzero flux with the canonical substitution
-i\boldsymbol{\nabla}\to\boldsymbol{\pi}
yielding

\displaystyle H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=v_{F}\tau_{0}(\mu_{3}\sigma_{1}\pi_{x}+\mu_{0}\sigma_{2}\pi_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mu_{0}\tau_{1}\cos 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}}+\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sin 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}

(317)

Eq.
317
is the central result of this section, making it a simple matter to determine the symmetries of the model at all flux, and then transform them into a momentum or Landau level basis. It is direct to check that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})\mathcal{T}^{-1}=H^{-\phi}(\mathbf{r})

(318)

because
\mathcal{T}(-i\boldsymbol{\nabla}+e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{r}))\mathcal{T}^{-1}=i\boldsymbol{\nabla}+e\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{r})
, so
-i\boldsymbol{\nabla}
and
\mathbf{A}
do not transform the same way unless the magnetic field is flipped. This is expected because a magnetic field is known to break time reversal.

The first and most important symmetry is
U(1)
valley
9
9

9

More carefully, TBG possesses a
U(2)\times U(2)
symmetry: spin and number conservation within each valley.
which is a continuous symmetry generated by
\mu_{3}
(note that
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
do not appear in Eq. (
317
) because the valleys are decoupled) which remains at all flux. There are three discrete symmetries of interest to us: inversion
C_{2z}
, unitary particle-hole
P
, and chiral symmetry
C
when
w_{0}=0
. All three survive in nonzero flux. The actions of these symmetries on the
full two-valley
Hamiltonian are

\displaystyle C_{2z}H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})C_{2z}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r}),\qquad C_{2z}^{2}=+1

(319)

\displaystyle PH^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})P^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=-H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r}),\qquad P^{2}=-1

\displaystyle CH^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})C

\displaystyle=-H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r}),\qquad C^{2}=+1,\qquad\text{when $w_{0}=0$}\ .

We now derive the forms of these symmetries in real space for all flux, momentum space at zero flux, and on the Landau level basis of the magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian at
2\pi
flux.

D.2.1
Real Space, all flux

We begin by proving the real-space form of the symmetries which can be derived from first principles in the continuum model. The results of this section alone are sufficient to check the physical symmetries of the BM model in flux. In App.
D.2.2
, we check that the same symmetries can also be written in momentum space, matching the expressions derived in Ref.
Song et al. 2019
.

To start, we analyze the
C_{2z}
which acts as a
\pi
rotation around the center of the graphene honeycomb unit cell. Hence, it takes
\mathbf{r}\to-\mathbf{r}
(switching the sublattice index) and hence inverts the momentum (switching the graphene valley).
C_{2z}
performs a rotation around the vector normal to the plane, and thus it does
not
act on the layer index. From this geometry, we arrive at

\displaystyle C_{2z}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\mu_{1}e^{i\pi L_{z}}

(320)

where
L_{z}
is the angular momentum operator given by

\displaystyle L_{z}=\frac{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{2}-\mathbf{Q}^{2}}{2eB}=-i\epsilon_{\mu\nu}x_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}

(321)

where in the second equality we used the symmetric gauge. By working in the symmetric gauge, one can see explicitly that the
L_{z}
operator is the same as at zero flux. However, we prefer to use the gauge-invariant
L_{z}
operator for the sake of generality. In this case it is easy to show in any gauge that the gauge-invariant inversion operator
e^{i\pi L_{z}}
reverses the canonical momentum

\displaystyle e^{i\pi L_{z}}\boldsymbol{\pi}e^{-i\pi L_{z}}=-\boldsymbol{\pi}\ .

(322)

We check directly that
D[C_{2z}]
is a symmetry by computing

\displaystyle C_{2z}H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})C_{2z}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\mu_{1}\left(-v_{F}\tau_{0}(\mu_{3}\sigma_{1}\pi_{x}+\mu_{0}\sigma_{2}\pi_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mu_{0}\tau_{1}\cos 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}}-\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sin 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}\right)\sigma_{1}\mu_{1}

(323)

\displaystyle=-v_{F}\tau_{0}(-\mu_{3}\sigma_{1}\partial_{x}-\mu_{0}\sigma_{2}\partial_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mu_{0}\tau_{1}\cos 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}}+\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sin 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})e^{(-1)^{2}\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-(-1)^{2}\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}

\displaystyle=H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})

which is the correct behavior for the two-valley Hamiltonian. We now discuss the unitary particle-hole symmetry
P
which acts as inversion in real space and also interchanges the layers, so it takes the form

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=i\tau_{2}\mu_{3}e^{i\pi L_{z}}\ .

(324)

The factor of
i
ensures
P
is real and commutes with
\mathcal{T}
. We check the action of
P
directly:

\displaystyle PH^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})P^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\tau_{2}\mu_{3}\left(-v_{F}\tau_{0}(\mu_{3}\sigma_{1}\pi_{x}+\mu_{0}\sigma_{2}\pi_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mu_{0}\tau_{1}\cos 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}}-\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sin 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}\right)\tau_{2}\mu_{3}

(325)

\displaystyle=-v_{F}\tau_{0}(\mu_{3}\sigma_{1}\pi_{x}+\mu_{0}\sigma_{2}\pi_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(-\mu_{0}\tau_{1}\cos 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}}-\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sin 2\pi{\bf{q}}_{j}\cdot{\bf{r}})e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}

\displaystyle=-H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})\ .

An identical calculation shows that
P^{\prime}=i\tau_{2}\mu_{0}e^{i\pi L_{z}}
(and thus any diagonal
\mu
matrix) also anti-commutes with
H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})
, which shows that
P
is not only an anti-commuting symmetry of the two-valley model, but also of each valley individually. This is due to the
U(1)
valley symmetry. Lastly, it is trivial to show that the chiral symmetry

\displaystyle C=\tau_{0}\sigma_{3}\mu_{0}

(326)

anti-commutes with
H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})
when
w_{0}=0
(the first chiral limit) because

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}T_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{3}

\displaystyle=w_{1}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}

(327)

\displaystyle=-w_{1}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}\sigma_{1}e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}(j-1)\mu_{3}\sigma_{3}}

and
\sigma_{3}
trivially anti-commutes with the kinetic term because it only contains
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
matrices. Note that
\tau_{0}\sigma_{3}\mu_{3}
also anti-commutes with
H^{\phi}(\mathbf{r})
, so
C
is an anti-commuting symmetry of both valleys individually.

Our calculations establish that
C_{2z}
,
P
, and the chiral symmetry
C
at
w_{0}=0
are unbroken by flux. Because
\mathcal{T}
reverses the flux,
C_{2z}\mathcal{T}
is not a symmetry at
\phi\neq 0
, which has a significant effect on the topology. Finally, it is direct to check that
\{P,C_{2z}\}=0
arising from the anti-commutation of the valley factors
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{3}
and hence
(C_{2z}P)^{2}=-P^{2}C_{2z}^{2}=+1
. The most important observation is that
C_{2z}P=\mu_{2}\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}
which is local in real space: the
e^{i\pi L_{z}}
operators have canceled.

D.2.2
Zero flux, Momentum space

Although it is not required for this work, we now connect the real space symmetry operators we have derived in App.
D.2.1
to the momentum space symmetries familiar in zero flux. We will follow heavily Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021c
.

First, let us ignore interlayer coupling in the BM model. The two sheets of graphene have four Dirac cones (two for layer and two for spin.) If the standard sheet of graphene has Dirac cones at
\tilde{{\bf{k}}}={\bf{K}}_{g},-{\bf{K}}_{g}
, then in twisted bilayer graphene they occur at
{\bf{K}}_{+}=M_{\theta/2}{\bf{K}}_{g},-{\bf{K}}_{+}=-M_{\theta/2}{\bf{K}}_{g},
for the top layer, and
{\bf{K}}_{-}=M_{-\theta/2}{\bf{K}}_{g},-{\bf{K}}_{-}=-M_{-\theta/2}{\bf{K}}_{g}
for the bottom. Here
M_{\pm\theta/2}
is a rotation matrix. Adding interlayer coupling reduces the graphene translation symmetry to the (approximate) moiré translation symmetry; momenta separated by reciprocal lattice vectors
{\bf{Q}}=2\pi m{\bf{b}}_{1}+2\pi n{\bf{b}}_{2}
become identified. In the folded moiré BZ, the four Dirac cones are located at the moiré
K
points
{\bf{K}}_{M}=\pi{\bf{q}}_{1},-{\bf{K}}_{M}=-\pi{\bf{q}}_{1}
which each host
2
Dirac cones (one per valley). Define the two
{\cal Q}
sublattices to be

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{+}

\displaystyle=\{\pi{\bf{q}}_{1}+2\pi m{\bf{b}}_{1}+2\pi n{\bf{b}}_{2}|m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}

(328)

\displaystyle{\cal Q}_{-}

\displaystyle=\{-\pi{\bf{q}}_{1}+2\pi m{\bf{b}}_{1}+2\pi n{\bf{b}}_{2}|m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

(329)

The two Dirac points
{\bf{K}}_{+},-{\bf{K}}_{-}\in{\cal Q}_{+}
, while
{\bf{K}}_{-},-{\bf{K}}_{+}\in{\cal Q}_{-}
. Thus, if we define

\displaystyle\zeta_{\bf{Q}}=\begin{cases}+1,~~{\bf{Q}}\in{\cal Q}_{+}\\
-1,~~{\bf{Q}}\in{\cal Q}_{-},\end{cases}

(330)

then
\zeta_{\bf{Q}}=\eta l
, with
\eta
as the valley and
l
the layer. We are now in a position to define the Fourier-transformed states.

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{Q}},\eta,\alpha,s}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}}\text{ in layer }l}e^{i({\bf{k}}+\eta{\bf{K}}_{l}-{\bf{Q}})\cdot(\tilde{{\bf{R}}}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\alpha})}c_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}},l,\alpha,s}^{\dagger},\qquad\text{with $\mathbf{Q},\eta,$ and $l$ obeying }\ l=\zeta_{\bf{Q}}\eta,

(331)

where
\boldsymbol{t}_{\alpha}
is the graphene sublattice vector,
\alpha
is the sublattice index,
l=\pm
is the layer,
\tilde{\mathbf{R}}
is the graphene Bravais lattice, and
s
is the spin. The decoupled valleys are indexed by
\eta=\pm
.

We now study the matrix form of the symmetry operators. Define the unitary component of the symmetry
g
as
D(g)
, where

\displaystyle gc_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{Q}},\eta,\alpha,s}^{\dagger}g^{-1}=c_{g{\bf{k}},{\bf{Q}}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}[D(g)]_{{\bf{Q}},\eta,\alpha,s,{\bf{Q}}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\ .

(332)

Applying
T
to Eq. (
331
) gives

\displaystyle{\cal T}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{Q}},\eta,\alpha,s}{\cal T}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}}\text{ in layer }l}e^{-i({\bf{k}}+\eta{\bf{K}}_{l}-{\bf{Q}})\cdot(\tilde{{\bf{R}}}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\alpha})}{\cal T}c_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}},l,\alpha,s}^{\dagger}{\cal T}^{-1}

(333)

\displaystyle=c^{\dagger}_{-{\bf{k}},-{\bf{Q}},-\eta,\alpha,s}

(334)

so
D[{\cal T}]=\delta_{{\bf{Q}},-{\bf{Q}}^{\prime}}\mu_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}
takes
{\bf{k}}\rightarrow-{\bf{k}}
. Because both
\zeta_{\bf{Q}}
and
\eta
change, the layer remains the same. This matches the real-space picture because
\mathcal{T}
is local.

A similar calculation holds for
C_{2z}
and
C
. For
C
, which only acts as
\pm 1
on the sublattice, define
\xi_{\alpha}=+1
for sublattice A and
-1
for sublattice B. Then

\displaystyle{C}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{Q}},\eta,\alpha,s}{C}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}}\text{ in layer }l}e^{-i({\bf{k}}+\eta{\bf{K}}_{l}-{\bf{Q}})\cdot(\tilde{{\bf{R}}}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\alpha})}{C}c_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}},l,\alpha,s}^{\dagger}{C}^{-1}

(335)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}}\text{ in layer }l}e^{-i({\bf{k}}+\eta{\bf{K}}_{l}-{\bf{Q}})\cdot(\tilde{{\bf{R}}}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\alpha})}c_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}},l,\alpha,s}^{\dagger}\xi_{\alpha}

(336)

so
D[{C}]=\sigma_{3}
and
C
takes
{\bf{k}}\rightarrow{\bf{k}}
. For
C_{2z}
, we use
\mathbf{t}_{\alpha}=-\mathbf{t}_{\beta}
when
\alpha\neq\beta
:

\displaystyle{C_{2z}}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},{\bf{Q}},\eta,\alpha,s}{C_{2z}}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}}\text{ in layer }l}e^{-i({\bf{k}}+\eta{\bf{K}}_{l}-{\bf{Q}})\cdot(\tilde{{\bf{R}}}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\alpha})}{C_{2z}}c_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}},l,\alpha,s}^{\dagger}{C_{2z}}^{-1}

(338)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}}\text{ in layer }l}e^{-i({\bf{k}}+\eta{\bf{K}}_{l}-{\bf{Q}})\cdot(\tilde{{\bf{R}}}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\alpha})}c_{\tilde{-{\bf{R}}},l,\beta,s}^{\dagger}[\sigma_{1}]_{\beta\alpha}

(339)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}}\text{ in layer }l}e^{-i({\bf{k}}+\eta{\bf{K}}_{l}-{\bf{Q}})\cdot(-\tilde{{\bf{R}}}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\beta})}c_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}},l,\beta,s}^{\dagger}[\sigma_{1}]_{\beta\alpha}

(340)

\displaystyle=c^{\dagger}_{-{\bf{k}},-{\bf{Q}},-\eta,\beta,s}[\sigma_{1}]_{\beta\alpha}

(341)

so
D[{C_{2z}}]=\delta_{{\bf{Q}},-{\bf{Q}}^{\prime}}\mu_{1}\sigma_{1}
and takes
{\bf{k}}\rightarrow-{\bf{k}}
. It is important to note that, like
\mathcal{T}
,
C_{2z}
switches the sign of valley and
\mathbf{Q}
, the layer remains the same. This matches the real-space picture because
C_{2z}
is a rotation in the plane, and does not reverse the layer.

The particle-hole symmetry
P
differs from
\mathcal{T}
and
C_{2z}
because
P
(which takes
\mathbf{R}\to-\mathbf{R}
and
l\to-l
as in Eq. (
324
)) is not a true symmetry of moiré superlattice
Song et al. 2019
, and only emerges when
\theta\to 0
in the continuum BM model. As such,
P
does not have a well-defined action on the
c_{\tilde{{\bf{R}}},l,\alpha,s}
because taking
\mathbf{R}\to-\mathbf{R}
and
l\to-l
is not an exact symmetry of the moiré lattice. We will simply give the momentum-space form of
P
in a single valley from Ref.
Song et al. 2019
, which is

\displaystyle D[P]=\zeta_{\bf{Q}}\delta_{{\bf{Q}},-{\bf{Q}}^{\prime}}\mu_{3},~{\bf{k}}\rightarrow-{\bf{k}}\ .

(343)

The crucial observation is that
D[P]
acts on the layer index
l
(determined by
\mathbf{Q}\in\eta\mathcal{Q}_{l}
) as
i\tau_{2}
because
\zeta_{\bf{Q}}
takes opposite values for opposite
l
and taking
\mathbf{Q}\to-\mathbf{Q}
reverses the layer. This exactly matches the real space symmetry derived in Eq. (
324
).

D.2.3
2\pi
-flux, Magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian

We now consider the magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian at
2\pi
flux, which is written as a matrix with layer, sublattice, and Landau level indices in a given valley. We derive the forms of the particle-hole
P
and inversion symmetries
C_{2z}
whose product
C_{2z}P
is a local symmetry of the two-valley Bistrizter-MacDonald (BM) Hamiltonian. Recall that at
\phi=2\pi
, the matrix Hamiltonian in the
K
graphene valley is

\displaystyle H^{\phi=2\pi}_{K}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}-\pi v_{F}\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}&T_{1}+T_{2}e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+T_{3}e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}e^{2\pi i\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+T_{3}e^{2\pi i\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}}&v_{F}\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\pi v_{F}\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\\
\end{pmatrix}\ .

(344)

which in the Landau level basis reads

\displaystyle H_{K}^{\phi=2\pi}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{2}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\end{pmatrix},\quad h(\boldsymbol{\pi})=\left(\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2\pi}\right)^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}0&a^{\dagger}\\
a&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\ .

(345)

We will also need an explicit expression for the magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian in the
K^{\prime}
valley which we obtained from Eq. (
314
), which in Bloch form reads

\displaystyle H^{\phi=2\pi}_{K^{\prime}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-v_{F}\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}+\pi v_{F}\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}&T_{1}+T_{2}^{*}e^{2\pi i\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+T_{3}^{*}e^{2\pi i\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}^{\prime}e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+T_{3}^{\prime}e^{-2\pi i\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}}&-v_{F}\boldsymbol{\pi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{*}-\pi v_{F}\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\\
\end{pmatrix}\ .

(346)

As explained in Eq. (
310
), we have performed a momentum shift between the two layers to account for the
-2\pi\mathbf{q}_{1}
displacement between the Dirac cones in the
K^{\prime}
valley (compared to a
+2\pi\mathbf{q}_{1}
displacement in the
K
valley).

Now following the same steps as in Eq. (
345
), we obtain the magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle H_{K^{\prime}}^{\phi=2\pi}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{2}^{*}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}^{*}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}^{*}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}^{*}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\end{pmatrix},\quad h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})=-\left(\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2\pi}\right)^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\
a^{\dagger}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\ .

(347)

In particular, the kinetic term is obtained via

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})

\displaystyle=-\pi_{x}\sigma_{x}+\pi_{y}\sigma_{y}

(348)

\displaystyle=-(\pi_{x}\sigma_{x}-\pi_{y}\sigma_{y})

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2\pi}\right)^{1/2}\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\
a^{\dagger}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{1}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})\sigma_{1}

and the potential term is merely complex conjugated (it does not have explicit flux dependence). The identities
T_{2}^{*}=T_{3},T_{3}^{*}=T_{2}
and
T_{1}^{*}=T_{1}
will be used throughout the section. We now derive the action of the continuum symmetry operators in Eq. (
D.2.1
) on the magnetic translation group irreps. The only nontrivial action is that of the rotation operator
e^{i\pi L_{z}}
which obeys

\displaystyle e^{i\pi L_{z}}\boldsymbol{\pi}e^{-i\pi L_{z}}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\pi},

(349)

\displaystyle e^{i\pi L_{z}}\mathbf{Q}e^{-i\pi L_{z}}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{Q},

and thus
\{e^{i\pi L_{z}},a^{\dagger}\}=0
because
a^{\dagger}=(\pi_{x}+i\pi_{y})/\sqrt{2eB}
. Acting on Eq. (
96
), we derive

\displaystyle e^{i\pi L_{z}}\ket{\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})}e^{i\pi L_{z}}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}e^{-i\pi L_{z}}e^{i\pi L_{z}}\ket{n}

(350)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot(-\mathbf{Q})}e^{i\pi L_{z}}\frac{a^{{\dagger}n}}{\sqrt{n!}}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}+i\frac{\phi}{2}(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot(-\mathbf{Q})}(-1)^{n}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(-\mathbf{R})+i\frac{\phi}{2}(-\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{1})(-\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{b}_{2})}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{Q}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\ket{-\mathbf{k},n}

which is expected because the Landau level state
\ket{n}
has angular momentum
n
. With this result, we can determine the action of the
C_{2z}
and
P
symmetries on the
\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
basis. It will be useful to note
(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}\ket{-\mathbf{k},n}=(-1)^{n}\ket{-\mathbf{k},n}
.

First we prove that particle-hole symmetry in the
K
valley is implemented by the operator

\displaystyle P_{K}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}=i\tau_{2}\sigma_{0}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a},\qquad[(-1)^{{a}^{\dagger}a}]_{mn}=(-1)^{m}\delta_{mn}

(351)

which is determined from Eq. (
324
) in real space using Eq. (
350
) to derive the Landau level parity operator
(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}
, which obeys
\{(-1)^{{a}^{\dagger}a},a\}=\{(-1)^{{a}^{\dagger}a},a^{\dagger}\}=0
because
[a^{\dagger}a,a]=-1
. We abuse notation and refer to
(-1)^{{a}^{\dagger}a}
as the matrix representation of the Landau level operators on the Landau level basis. Thus
(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}
anti-commutes with
h(\boldsymbol{\pi})
which is linear in
a,a^{\dagger}
. We also need
(-1)^{{a}^{\dagger}a}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}(-1)^{{a}^{\dagger}a}=\mathcal{H}^{-\mathbf{q}}
as is apparent from Eq. (
223
). Using these results, we have

\displaystyle P_{K}H_{K}^{\phi=2\pi}(\mathbf{k})P_{K}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{2}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

(352)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&-(T_{1}+T_{2}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}})\\
-(T_{1}+T_{2}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}})&v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{2}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-H_{K}^{\phi=2\pi}(-\mathbf{k})\ .

We now study the
K^{\prime}
valley where particle-hole is implemented by the operator

\displaystyle P_{K^{\prime}}=-\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}=-i\tau_{2}\sigma_{0}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}

(353)

which differs from Eq. (
351
) only by an overall minus. We are free to choose the sign of
P
independently in each valley because of the
U(1)
valley symmetry. We check Eq. (
351
) directly following the same steps in Eq. (
352
):

\displaystyle P_{K^{\prime}}H_{K^{\prime}}^{\phi=2\pi}(\mathbf{k})P_{K^{\prime}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{2}^{*}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}^{*}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{+2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}^{*}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}^{*}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

(354)

\displaystyle=-\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{2}^{*}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}^{*}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}^{*}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}^{*}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-H_{K^{\prime}}^{\phi=2\pi}(-\mathbf{k})\ .

Thus we have checked the particle-hole operator in both valleys, yielding the final expression

\displaystyle P=\mu_{3}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}=i\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sigma_{0}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}

(355)

where
\mu_{3}
is a Pauli matrix on the valley index. The fact that we can choose the
\pm
sign freely in different valleys is a feature of the
U(1)
valley quantum number. The same is true in real space as in Eq. (
324
).

We now study
C_{2z}
which is not a symmetry of the one-valley BM model because it reverses the valley. Using Eq. (
350
), the expression for
C_{2z}
on the Landau level basis is

\displaystyle C_{2z}=\mu_{1}\tau_{0}\sigma_{1}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}

(356)

which acts trivially on the layer indices. We now compute the action of
C_{2z}
on
H_{K}^{\phi=2\pi}
directly:

\displaystyle C_{2z}H^{\phi=2\pi}_{K}(\mathbf{k})C_{2z}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{2}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{2}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{3}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\end{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}

(357)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}\sigma_{1}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})\sigma_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})&T_{1}+T_{3}e^{-ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{2}e^{ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{-2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}\\
T_{1}+T_{3}e^{ik_{2}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{1}}+T_{2}e^{-ik_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{2\pi\mathbf{b}_{2}}&v_{F}k_{\theta}(-\sqrt{\frac{\phi}{2\pi}}\sigma_{1}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})\sigma_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2})\end{pmatrix}

where we used that
\sigma_{1}T_{2}\sigma_{1}=T_{3},\sigma_{1}T_{3}\sigma_{1}=T_{2}
. Comparing Eq. (
357
) with Eq. (
347
) and using
h^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\pi})=-\sigma_{1}h(\boldsymbol{\pi})\sigma_{1}
, we find that
C_{2z}H_{K}(\mathbf{k})C_{2z}^{\dagger}=H_{K^{\prime}}(-\mathbf{k})
and thus

\displaystyle C_{2z}

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}\tau_{0}\sigma_{1}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a}

(358)

which anti-commutes with
P
due to the valley matrices.

Lastly, we study the chiral symmetry on the
\ket{\mathbf{k},n}
basis. Chiral symmetry is very simple because it acts trivially on
\mathbf{r}
(see Eq. (
326
)). It is direct to see that when
w_{0}=0
, the chiral symmetry

\displaystyle C_{K}=C_{K^{\prime}}=\tau_{0}\sigma_{3}\mathbb{1}

(359)

(where
\mathbb{1}
acts on the Landau levels) obeys

\displaystyle\{C_{K},H^{\phi=2\pi}_{K}(\mathbf{k})\}=\{C_{K^{\prime}},H^{\phi=2\pi}_{K^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})\}=0,

(360)

because Eqs. (
345
) and (
346
) have only
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
matrices when
w_{0}=0
. Thus we can choose

\displaystyle C=\mu_{0}\tau_{0}\sigma_{3}\mathbb{1}

(361)

to be the chiral symmetry on the two-valley model. Again, we emphasize that any diagonal
\mu
matrix is allowed, and the choice of
\mu_{0}
carries no physical significance. This is because of the
U(1)
valley symmetry.

The most important symmetry for the many-body properties of TBG is
C_{2z}P
which acts as
(\mu_{1}\tau_{0}\sigma_{1}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a})(i\mu_{3}\tau_{2}\sigma_{0}(-1)^{a^{\dagger}a})=\mu_{2}\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}\mathbb{1}
on the magnetic translation group eigenbasis (see Eq. (
356
) and Eq. (
355
)) and takes
\mathbf{k}\to\mathbf{k}
.

D.3
Coulomb Interaction at
\phi=2\pi

We now study interaction effects within the flat bands. We will neglect the Zeeman splitting which is
\leq 2
meV. Although this is comparable to the flat band kinetic energy, the gap to the passive bands is
\geq 40
meV which justifies a strong coupling expansion where the single-particle flat bands are taken to be exactly flat at zero energy. In this case, there is an
SU(2)
spin symmetry, and and an obvious
U(1)
charge symmetry. Together, the
U(1)
charge symmetry,
U(1)
valley symmetry, the
SU(2)
spin symmetry, and the product
C_{2z}P
symmetry are all preserved at nonzero flux. These symmetries are essential in the many-body physics of TBG as we now discuss.

First, we set up the many-body notation. Explicitly, the Hilbert space is spanned by the operators
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,s,\eta}
where
M=\pm 1
refers to the two flat bands,
s
to the spin, and
\eta
to the valley. The wavefunction of the
M
th band is

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbf{r},s,\eta|\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,s,\eta}|0}=\sum_{l\alpha n}U_{l\alpha n}^{M,\eta}(\mathbf{k})\psi_{\mathbf{k},l,\alpha,n},\quad\psi_{\mathbf{k},l,\alpha,n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{k})}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}T_{\mathbf{R}}\frac{a^{{\dagger}n}}{\sqrt{n!}}\psi_{0,l,\alpha}(\mathbf{r})

(362)

where
U_{l\alpha n}^{M,\eta}(\mathbf{k})
are the matrix eigenvectors of the single-particle Hamiltonian in the
\eta
valley and
\psi_{0,l,\alpha}
is the zeroth Landau level state in the
l=\pm 1
layer and
\alpha=A,B
sublattice. Note that the single-particle Hamiltonian is explicitly periodic:
H^{\phi=2\pi}(\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G})=H^{\phi=2\pi}(\mathbf{k})
and the states
\psi_{\mathbf{k},l,\alpha,n}
are also explicitly periodic, so
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G},M,s,\eta}=\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,s,\eta}
. The two valleys are related by
C_{2z}P=\mu_{2}\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}\mathbb{1}
(see App.
D.2.3
), where
\mathbb{1}
acts on the Landau level indices and obeys

\displaystyle-H^{\phi=2\pi}_{-\eta}(\mathbf{k})=(i\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}\mathbb{1})^{\dagger}H^{\phi=2\pi}_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})(i\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}\mathbb{1})\ .

(363)

which relates the Hamiltonians between the two valleys. Note that
C_{2z}P
acts trivially on the Landau level indices.

We will now use
C_{2z}P
to relate the eigenvectors of the two valleys. We focus on the two flat bands which we index by
M=\pm
. We denote by
U^{\eta}(\mathbf{k})
the matrix of occupied eigenvectors where each column
U_{N}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k})
is one of the flat band eigenvectors in the
\eta
valley. Because
C_{2z}P
anti-commutes with the single-particle Hamiltonian at each
\mathbf{k}
,
C_{2z}P
switches the energies of the flat bands and we have

\displaystyle i\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}\mathbb{1}U_{N}^{\eta=K}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{M}U^{\eta=-K}_{M}(\mathbf{k})[\nu_{1}]_{MN}

(364)

where
\nu_{1}
is a Pauli matrix which exchanges the two bands. Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021c
used
\xi_{i}
to represent these Pauli matrices, but we reserve
\xi
for
\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})
the phase fact. One can think of Eq. (
364
) as a
C_{2z}P
gauge-fixing procedure for the eigenstates equivalent to the sewing matrices of Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021c
. We point out that the sewing matrices were vital to constructing the Chern basis at zero flux in Refs.
Bernevig et al. 2021c
;
Lian et al. 2021b
which is protected by
C_{2z}\mathcal{T}
. In the gauge fixing of Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021c
, it was convenient to set the sewing matrix of
C_{2z}\mathcal{T}
to be the identity. However at
2\pi
flux, we will not need to make use of the sewing matrices because
C_{2z}\mathcal{T}
is broken by the flux and there is no Chern basis.

Note that the Hamiltonian is independent of the spin, so the eigenstates at different spin are identical. Hence we adapt the form factor from Eq. (
299
) to TBG in the
\eta
valley via

\displaystyle\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\equiv e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}[U_{\eta}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})(\tau_{0}\sigma_{0}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}})U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})]_{MN}\ .

(365)

As explained in Eq. (
47
),
\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
is not gauge-invariant because the eigenvectors in the columns of
U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})
and
U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})
are only defined up to an overall phase (or arbitrary unitary transformations at band touchings), meaning
U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})\to U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})V
and
U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})\to U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})W
where
V
and
W
are arbitrary
2\times 2
unitary matrices at each
\mathbf{k}
. Hence
\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
is only defined up to the eigenvector gauge transformations
\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\to W^{\dagger}\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})V
. Under this transformation, only the singular values of
\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
are gauge-invariant. The singular values of
M
are the eigenvalues of
M^{\dagger}M
, which are invariant because
M^{\dagger}M\to V^{\dagger}M^{\dagger}WW^{\dagger}MV=V^{\dagger}M^{\dagger}MV
has the same spectrum as
M^{\dagger}M
. There is notable simplification at
\mathbf{q}=2\pi\mathbf{G}
where

\displaystyle\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\equiv e^{i\xi_{2\pi\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{k})}[U_{\eta}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})(\tau_{0}\sigma_{0}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}})U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})]_{MN}

(366)

using the fact that
U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})=U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k}+2\pi\mathbf{G})
because the magnetic Bloch Hamiltonian
H^{\phi=2\pi}(\mathbf{k})
is explicitly
2\pi\mathbf{G}
periodic. In this case
\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
is defined up to the eigenvector gauge transformation
\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\to V^{\dagger}\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})V
, and hence its
eigenvalues
are gauge-invariant.

For brevity, we will not write the identity factors
\tau_{0}\sigma_{0}
going forward. Because the valleys are related by
C_{2z}P
symmetry, Eq. (
364
) shows (writing
\eta
as a subscript for convenience)

\displaystyle\bar{M}^{-\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}U_{-\eta}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}U_{-\eta}(\mathbf{k})

(367)

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}U_{\eta}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}\tau_{2}\sigma_{1}U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})\nu_{1}

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}e^{i\xi_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{k})}U_{\eta}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{q}}U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})\nu_{1}

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\nu_{1}

so we see that
\bar{M}^{-\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
is related to
\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
by a unitary transformation.

The symmetries of
H_{int}
are essential to understanding its groundstates. The
U(1)
charge conversation and
SU(2)
spin rotation in each valley give a
U(2)\times U(2)
symmetry group that commutes with
O_{\mathbf{q}}
. The two copies of
U(2)
in separate valleys form
U(4)
when the
C_{2z}P
symmetry (which is not broken by magnetic field) is added to the symmetry group because
C_{2z}P
interchanges the valleys. We refer the reader to Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021c
for a comprehensive treatment of the
U(4)
algebra and irreps. Note that
C_{2z}P
takes
\mathbf{k}
to
(-1)^{2}\mathbf{k}
and so it commutes with Fourier modes of the density operator at all momenta. (The anti-unitary symmetries
C_{2z}\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}
which broken in flux are not part of the
U(4)
algebra.) The
U(4)
symmetry of
H_{int}
is a symmetry of the full Hamiltonian as well if we set the single particle Hamiltonian
H_{0}
to zero —otherwise
C_{2z}P
anti-commutes with
H_{0}
. In general, incorporating kinetic energy will split the
U(4)
irreps into
U(2)\times U(2)
irreps which an energy difference on the order of the bandwidth
\sim 1
meV.

We now define the Coulomb interaction in terms of the charge density
\bar{n}(\mathbf{r})
where the bar indicates that the density is measured with respect to charge neutrality. This is equivalent to choosing the chemical potential of the system at half-filling. Discretizing
\mathbf{k}
for numerical convenience, the density modes are

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}r\,e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\bar{n}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{MN,\eta,s}\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\left(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N,\eta,s}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{MN}\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}\right)\ .

(368)

Note that
\mathbf{k}
is summed over the moiré BZ
k_{i}\in(-\pi,\pi)
and
\mathbf{q}
is an arbitrary momentum in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The
\frac{1}{2}\delta_{MN}\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}
term shifts the eigenvalues of the density operator from
\{0,1\}
to
\{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\}
.
From Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2021c
, the Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H_{int}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{2}rd^{2}r^{\prime}\,\bar{n}(\mathbf{r})V(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})\bar{n}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{2}q}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,V(\mathbf{q})\bar{\rho}_{-\mathbf{q}}\bar{\rho}_{\mathbf{q}},\quad\ V(\mathbf{q})=\pi\xi^{2}U_{\xi}\frac{\tanh\xi|\mathbf{q}|/2}{\xi|\mathbf{q}|/2}

(369)

where the parameters of the screened Coulomb interaction are
\xi=10
nm,
U_{\xi}=e^{2}/(\epsilon\xi)=24
meV where
\epsilon
is the dielectric constant. Because
\mathbf{K}_{g}\gg 1/\xi
, intervalley scattering is strongly suppressed which justifies our decoupling of the valleys.

To understand the phase of TBG at fillings
\nu\in(-4,4)
where
H_{int}
describes the leading order electronic behavior, one must solve a strongly interacting problem. Our strategy to do so is to project
H_{int}
into the flat bands. This is straightforwardly done by keeping only the terms with
M=\pm 1
in Eq. (
368
).

Finally, we give the expression for the full Coulomb Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle H_{int}=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}}V(\mathbf{q})\bar{\rho}_{-\mathbf{q}}\bar{\rho}_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in BZ}O_{-\mathbf{q},-\mathbf{G}}O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}},

(370)

\displaystyle O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{V(\mathbf{q}+2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{\eta,s}\sum_{MN}\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}+2\pi\mathbf{G})(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N,\eta,s}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{MN}\delta_{\mathbf{q},0})\ .

It is now a simple matter to project
H_{int}
into the flat bands by restricting the sum to
M,N=\pm 1
, the two approximately zero energy flat bands. To good approximation, the single particle Hamiltonian vanishes when projected to the flat bands because the bandwidth is
<2
meV in comparison to the
26
meV scale of the screened Coulomb interaction. Thus, the low energy Hamiltonian consists entirely of the projected
H_{int}
operator.

D.4
Exact Insulator Groundstates

To derive eigenstates of the interacting Hamiltonian as in Ref.
Lian et al. 2021b
, we rewrite
H_{int}
by introducing a parameter
\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}
:

\displaystyle H_{int}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in BZ}O_{-\mathbf{q},-\mathbf{G}}O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}

(371)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\left(\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}O_{0,\mathbf{G}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}O_{0,-\mathbf{G}}-\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}+\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in BZ}(O_{-\mathbf{q},-\mathbf{G}}-\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}\delta_{\mathbf{q},0})(O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}-\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}\delta_{\mathbf{q},0})\right)

for any
\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}
which satisfies the Hermiticity condition
\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}=\lambda^{*}_{-\mathbf{G}}
. The purpose of introducing
\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}
is to make use of the flat metric condition
Bernevig et al. 2021b
which is the approximation

\displaystyle\text{flat metric condition: }\quad\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})=m_{\mathbf{G}}\delta_{MN},

(372)

in other words that
\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
is proportional to the
2\times 2
identity at each
\mathbf{G}
. (Importantly, Eq. (
366
) shows that the flat metric condition is “gauge-invariant” under unitary rescalings of the eigenvectors.) If the flat metric condition is satisfied, we will be able to analytically construct groundstates of
H_{int}
as in Ref.
Lian et al. 2021b
. To explain this, let us study
O_{0,\mathbf{G}}
which acts diagonally on
\mathbf{k}
:

\displaystyle O_{0,\mathbf{G}}=\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{\eta,s}\sum_{MN}\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N,\eta,s}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{MN})\ .

(373)

Because
O_{-\mathbf{q},-\mathbf{G}}=O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}^{\dagger}
, the term
\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}O_{0,\mathbf{G}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}O_{0,-\mathbf{G}}
in Eq. (
371
) is Hermitian and acts diagonally on
\mathbf{k}
. If the flat metric condition in Eq. (
372
) is satisfied, then

\displaystyle O_{0,\mathbf{G}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{\eta,s}\sum_{MN}m_{\mathbf{G}}\delta_{MN}(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N,\eta,s}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{MN})

(374)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{\eta,s}m_{\mathbf{G}}(\sum_{M}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}-1)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{\eta,s}m_{\mathbf{G}}(\sum_{M}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}-1)

which is proportional to the total particle number
N=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{\eta,s,M}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}
. This will be important for computing the chemical potential in Eq. (
384
). In this case, the only nontrivial part of
H_{int}
is the final
\mathbf{q}
sum in Eq. (
371
), which is also positive semi-definite. Because it is positive semi-definite, any state
\ket{\Psi}
satisfying

\displaystyle(O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}-\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}\delta_{\mathbf{q},0})\ket{\Psi}

\displaystyle=0

(375)

for some
\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}
is a groundstate. Note that states
\ket{\Psi}
satisfying Eq. (
375
) are still eigenstates of
O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}
and hence
H_{int}
without the flat metric condition, but we cannot prove they are groundstates because
\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}O_{0,\mathbf{G}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}O_{0,-\mathbf{G}}
is not in general proportional to the identity.
10
10

10

Note that the flat metric condition in Eq. (
372
) can be weakened to the requirement that
\sum_{\mathbf{G}}(\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}O_{0,\mathbf{G}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}O_{0,-\mathbf{G}})\propto N
up to constant terms.
As shown in Fig.
10
, that the flat metric condition holds to excellent accuracy for all
\mathbf{G}
except the first shell
\mathbf{G}=\pm\mathbf{b}_{1},\pm\mathbf{b}_{2},\pm(\mathbf{b}_{1}-\mathbf{b}_{2})
. On these momenta, the flat metric condition is only weakly violated. Hence the flat metric condition is a reliable approximation, and we can justify that the exact eigenstates in Eq. (
375
) are in fact groundstates at fixed filling.

We now construct states satisfying Eq. (
375
) at integer even density
\nu=-4,-2,0,2,4
around the charge neutrality point. Our trial state with
\nu+4
occupied flat bands takes the form

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\mathbf{k}}\prod_{j}^{(\nu+4)/2}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},+,s_{j},\eta_{j}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},-,s_{j},\eta_{j}}\ket{0}

(376)

where the spin and valley indices are arbitrary and is implicit in the state. The operator
O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}
has a simple action on
\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}
because

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N,\eta,s}\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}=0\text{ if }\mathbf{q}\neq 0

(377)

because both
M=\pm 1
bands are fully occupied or unoccupied at every
\mathbf{k}
. We also use the fact

\displaystyle\sum_{MN}\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N,\eta,s}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},+,\eta,s}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},-,\eta,s}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{MN}\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}(\delta_{N,+}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},-,\eta,s}-\delta_{N,-}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},+,\eta,s})\ket{0}

(378)

\displaystyle=\sum_{M}(\bar{M}^{\eta}_{M+}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},-,\eta,s}-\bar{M}^{\eta}_{M-}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},+,\eta,s})\ket{0}

\displaystyle=(\bar{M}^{\eta}_{++}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},+,\eta,s}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},-,\eta,s}-\bar{M}^{\eta}_{--}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},-,\eta,s}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},+,\eta,s})\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\ [\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})]\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},+,\eta,s}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},-,\eta,s}\ket{0}

which we use to calculate (with
\mathbf{q}\in BZ
and recalling that
\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}
contains flavors
s_{j}
and
\eta_{j}
)

\displaystyle O_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{G}}\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{\eta,s}\sum_{MN}\bar{M}^{\eta}_{MN}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},M,\eta,s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},N,\eta,s}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{MN})\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

(379)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\sum_{\eta,s}(\sum_{j}\delta_{s,s_{j}}\delta_{\eta,\eta_{j}}\text{Tr}\ [\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})]-\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\ [\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})])\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}(\frac{\nu+4}{2}\text{Tr}\ [\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})]-\frac{4}{2}\text{Tr}\ [\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})])\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\nu\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

where we used that
\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}^{-\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})=\text{Tr}\ \nu_{1}\bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\nu_{1}=\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})
. We use the abbreviation

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})=\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}^{-\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})=\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})

(380)

to emphasize that the trace is independent of the valley. Consulting Eq. (
375
), we find that
\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}
is an exact eigenstate provided we choose
Lian et al. 2021b

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}=\nu\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\ .

(381)

Using Eq. (
300
), we find
\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}=\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}^{*}
. Returning to Eq. (
371
) and acting on the state
\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}
, we find

\displaystyle H_{int}\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}O_{0,\mathbf{G}}\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}O_{0,-\mathbf{G}}\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}-\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

(382)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}-\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}\right)\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}\right)\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}

which gives the exact energy of the state (not including the chemical potential):

\displaystyle\frac{E_{int}}{\Omega_{tot}}=\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}V(2\pi\mathbf{G})\left|\frac{1}{\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\right|^{2}\ .

(383)

The dominant contribution is from the
\mathbf{G}=0
term where
\text{Tr}\ \bar{M}^{\eta}(\mathbf{k},0)=\text{Tr}\ U_{\eta}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})U_{\eta}(\mathbf{k})=2
. Numerically calculating the sums at
\mathbf{G}\neq 0
, we find that only the first shell of
\mathbf{G}
consisting of
\pm\mathbf{b}_{1},\pm\mathbf{b}_{2},\pm(\mathbf{b}_{1}-\mathbf{b}_{2})
contributes significantly to the sum due to the exponential fall-off of
M(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})
with
\mathbf{q}
. In general, we cannot guarantee that
\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}
is the groundstate without the flat metric condition. This assumption can be tested using exact diagonalization studies for small systems
2020arXiv201000588X
, which we leave for future work. However,
E_{int}=0
at
\nu=0
so it must be a groundstate because
H_{int}
is positive semi-definite.

If the FMC holds such that
M(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})=m_{\mathbf{G}}\mathbb{1}
, we can obtain an exact expression for the chemical potential
\mu
at even fillings. Using Eq. (
374
), we compute

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\left(\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}O_{0,\mathbf{G}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}O_{0,-\mathbf{G}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{tot}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}m_{\mathbf{G}}\right)N+h.c.+\text{const}=\mu N+\text{const},

(384)

where the chemical potential
\mu
is given by

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Omega_{tot}}\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\sqrt{V(2\pi\mathbf{G})}(\lambda_{-\mathbf{G}}m_{\mathbf{G}}+\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}m_{-\mathbf{G}})/2

(385)

\displaystyle=\nu\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\Omega^{-1}V(2\pi\mathbf{G})(m_{-\mathbf{G}}m_{\mathbf{G}}+m_{\mathbf{G}}m_{-\mathbf{G}})/2

\displaystyle=\nu\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\Omega^{-1}V(2\pi\mathbf{G})|m_{\mathbf{G}}|^{2}

and we used Eq. (
381
) and the fact that
\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\ M(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})=m_{\mathbf{G}}
. Note that
\mu=0
at
\nu=0
with or without the FMC because
\lambda_{\mathbf{G}}\propto\nu
. Because
H_{int}-\mu N
is positive definite (see Eq. (
371
)), we can guarantee
\ket{\Psi_{\nu}}
are many-body groundstates at chemical potential
\mu
in the FMC. If we do not make the FMC approximation, then we cannot determine an exact expression for the chemical potential. The approximation we make is to compute an average value of
m_{\mathbf{G}}
over the BZ:

\displaystyle\mu\approx\nu\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\Omega^{-1}V(2\pi\mathbf{G})\left|\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in BZ}\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\ M(\mathbf{k},2\pi\mathbf{G})\right|^{2}

(386)

which reduces to Eq. (
385
) if the FMC holds.
